- Every toggle that was on (LIVE toggles excepted: their state is a byte the game and its save file own, so there is nothing for qwark to re-apply), every active freeze, every client patch and every loaded mod is moved into the **previous-session record** and cleared from the live tables. Items flagged auto (toggle_auto, mod_auto) are re-applied silently when the session reaches INGAME and are removed from the record. What remains is config.txt's `reboot.apply`'s (revision 1.33, section 5.10.4): under Ask, the default, `flags.PREVIOUS_PENDING` is set and the client is expected to prompt the user (default No) and answer with PREVIOUS_REAPPLY or PREVIOUS_DISMISS, but with no client connected at that moment the record is dropped; under Always qwark re-applies it itself, as a PREVIOUS_REAPPLY of every category the platform can do; under Never it is dropped. Only Ask with a client connected leaves the flag set.

When a different title comes back, everything including watches is dropped and the record is empty. The client notices `game` changing in telemetry and re-issues DESCRIBE.

## 5. Opcodes

### 5.1 Session (0x00xx)

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0001 | HELLO | `u8 client_protocol_version` | SessionInfo, then `u32 build_id` (revision 1.24, section 3.1.1; a module before it sends SessionInfo alone) |
| 0x0002 | HEARTBEAT | none | none. Any frame counts as a heartbeat for the telemetry subscription; this one exists for idle clients |
| 0x0003 | NOTIFY | text, at most 255 bytes, no terminator | none. Shows an XMB notification |
| 0x0004 | PREVIOUS_LIST | none | `u64 toggles, u32 mods, u8 nfreeze, { u8 size, u8 pad[3], u32 addr, u64 value }[nfreeze], u8 npatch, { u32 first_addr, u16 nwords, u16 pad }[npatch]` |
| 0x0005 | PREVIOUS_REAPPLY | `u8 flags` bit0 toggles, bit1 mods, bit2 freezes, bit3 patches | none. Re-applies the chosen categories, then clears the record |
| 0x0006 | PREVIOUS_DISMISS | none | none. Clears the record |
| 0x0007 | VSH_MEMORY | none | 28 bytes, the VSH's free memory as the module last read it (build 54, section 5.1.1). `UNSUPPORTED` where it cannot be read |
| 0x0008 | GAME_MODULE | none | 40 bytes, the running game's module and the memory gate in front of it (revision 1.26, section 5.1.2) |

### 5.1.1 VSH_MEMORY, the VSH's free memory (build 54)

A VSH plugin lives in the VSH's own memory: its image and its thread stacks come out of the same pool as every other plugin's and the XMB's, and when that pool runs dry the console says "VSH out of memory" or reboots. The module reads how much of it is free with lv2 syscall 352, `sys_memory_get_user_memory_size`, which is read-only and answers for the calling process (webMAN's `/cpursx.ps3` shows the same number). It allocates nothing to do so.

**When it reads.** Each reading is one syscall.

| `at` | When |
|---|---|
| 1 START | module start, once its tables and sockets are up, just before the tick thread is created |
| 2 TICK_THREAD | just after the tick thread is created; the step from START is the thread's 16 KB stack, which shows the reading sees the module's own allocations |
| 3 GAME | a game reaches INGAME |
| 4 MENU | the console menu's renderer has gone into the game (section 9.3) |
| 5 PERIODIC | every ten seconds (1200 ticks) at the XMB and in a game. Never in BOOTING, its quiet second included, and never in QUITTING: a launch is left alone |

It keeps the latest reading, the lowest since the module started (with when, at what and in which game), and START's figure.

**The reply**, all big-endian:

```
u32 free           bytes free at the latest reading
u32 total          the VSH's user memory, in bytes, at that reading
u32 lowest         the lowest free since the module started, in bytes
u32 start          bytes free at START; 0 when that reading failed
u32 age_s          seconds since the latest reading (at most about ten at the XMB and in a game)
u32 lowest_age_s   seconds since the lowest reading
u8  lowest_at      what the lowest reading was taken at, the table above
u8  lowest_game    SessionInfo's `game` byte at the lowest reading: 0 at the XMB, or in a title no game table claims
u8  pad[2]         0
```

Ages are counted in whole seconds of the console's clock. A client reads `n` bytes and takes the fields it knows; a later revision only ever adds at the end.

**When it answers.** `OK` with the 28 bytes once a reading has succeeded, which is what SessionInfo `flags` bit5 VSH_MEMORY says (section 3): on a console from module start. `UNSUPPORTED` where nothing can be read: `qwark-rpcs3.exe` always, since RPCS3's memory is not a VSH's, and the `qwark-host.exe` simulator until it is given a figure (below). A module before build 54 answers `UNKNOWN_OP` and never sets bit5, so a client that asks only while bit5 is set never meets either refusal. VSH_MEMORY is a control op (section 1.1): it takes none of the shared request buffers and is answered in every session state, BOOTING and QUITTING included. It answers from what the module kept, asking the VSH nothing (it reads only the clock, for the readings' ages), so a client may poll it; every ten seconds matches the readings.

**qwark.log.** Every line starts `vsh mem:`, so `grep "vsh mem"` finds them all:

```
vsh mem: 21312 KB free of 39936 KB (start, 9 us)
vsh mem: 21296 KB free of 39936 KB (tick thread, 8 us)
vsh mem: 1880 KB free of 31744 KB (NPEA00423, 8 us)     a game reached INGAME
vsh mem: 1864 KB free of 31744 KB (menu, 8 us)          its menu went in
vsh mem: 960 KB free of 31744 KB (low, 8 us)            a ten-second reading, below
vsh mem: lowest 960 KB free in NPEA00423                the game's QUITTING: its own lowest
```

KB is 1024 bytes, rounded down. The microseconds are how long the syscall took. START, TICK_THREAD, GAME and MENU are always logged. A ten-second reading is logged only as a **low**: when it is under **1 MB** free, and at least 64 KB (one page) under every figure a line has carried before, so a VSH that sits near the edge writes a handful of lines and not one every ten seconds. 1 MB is four times the 256 KB under which webMAN stops serving pages; a module load or an XMB notification can each take a good part of it. The QUITTING line is the lowest of the readings taken while that game was INGAME, its arrival included. `log = 0` silences these as it does every line (section 5.10).

**The simulator.** `qwark-host.exe` has nothing to read until it is given a figure: `--vshmem FREE,TOTAL` on its command line, in KB, gives START and TICK_THREAD one, and the stdin command `vshmem <free KB> <total KB>` sets it and takes a reading at once, as a ten-second one; `vshmem off` makes it unreadable again (what it has kept stays).

### 5.1.2 GAME_MODULE, the running game's module (revision 1.26)

What section 9.11's gate is doing, for a client to say in plain words. A control op (section 1.1), answered in every state from what the tick last wrote, so a client may poll it while `flags2` bit5 or bit6 is set. All big-endian:

```
u8  state        0 NONE      no game: the XMB, BOOTING, a title no game claims
                 1 LINKED    the game is linked into the module (build 56's release, qwark-rpcs3)
                 2 CHECKING  a module is wanted and its file not yet read (a tick at most)
                 3 WAITING   the module is there; the gate holds it back       (flags2 bit6)
                 4 LOADED    loaded and started
                 5 MISSING   no module file (rac1.sprx ..)                     (flags2 bit5)
                 6 FAILED    no module in the file, or lv2 would not load or start it (bit5)
                 7 OTHER     the module is another build's                     (flags2 bit5)
u8  game         the session's game by its title (0 none), whose module is the file of section 9.11's table
u8  pages        the module's 64 KB pages, from its header; 0 not read
u8  flags        bit0 KEEP: config.txt's game.keep is 1
                 bit1 NOTIFIED: this wait's 30-second notification has gone
                 bit2 MODULES: this module's games are modules (a test build, and a release
                      from build 57; 0 for build 56's release and qwark-rpcs3)
                 bit3 RESIDENT: a module is loaded now (a kept one included)
u32 lowest_kb    the lowest free figure over the window at the gate's last look, KB
u32 now_kb       the latest reading, KB
u32 need_kb      what a load needs: the pages and the 64 KB transient, KB
u32 floor_kb     game.floor_kb
u32 window_ms    game.window_ms
u32 waited_ms    WAITING: how long so far; else 0
u32 error        FAILED: lv2's error (0x8001xxxx), 0x800000nn for a start that refused with
                 reason nn, 0x80000100 for a table that was not the module's own game's, 0 for
                 a file that is no module; OTHER: the reason, 2 another build number, 3 another
                 build ID, 4 another core table; else 0
u32 load_us      how long the last load and start took
u32 module_id    OTHER: the build ID the module says it is
```

The gate's sum is `lowest_kb - need_kb >= floor_kb`. A client that wants to say how much more the console needs takes `need_kb + floor_kb - lowest_kb`. A later revision only adds at the end.

### 5.2 Telemetry (0x001x)

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0010 | SUBSCRIBE | `u16 udp_port` | none. Packets go to the connection's remote address and this port. The subscription ends when the connection closes. A subscriber that has sent no frame for 5 s is not sent to, and its next frame of any kind starts the packets again |
| 0x0011 | UNSUBSCRIBE | none | none |
| 0x0012 | GET_STATE | none | the telemetry packet, magic included |

### 5.3 Features (0x002x)

Each game exposes up to 64 features with stable ids. Kinds: 0 TOGGLE, 1 ACTION, 2 VALUE (u32, or two's complement when the row is flagged SIGNED, section 5.3.2), 3 ENUM, 4 COLOR (`0x00RRGGBB`).

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0020 | DESCRIBE | none | `u8 game, u8 ngroups, char[24] group[ngroups], u8 nreadouts, char[24] readout[nreadouts], u8 nfeatures, Feature[nfeatures]`. At most 16 groups, 16 readouts, 64 features |
| 0x0021 | FEATURE_SET | `u8 id, u32 value` (TOGGLE: 0 or 1; SIGNED VALUE: the low `bits` bits, section 5.3.2) | none. Works on a LIVE toggle too: it writes the byte |
| 0x0022 | FEATURE_TRIGGER | `u8 id` (ACTION only) | none |
| 0x0023 | FEATURE_SET_AUTO | `u8 id, u8 auto`, and on a COLOR (revision 1.24) `u32 value` | none. Persisted in config. UNSUPPORTED on a LIVE toggle, BAD_ARG on an ACTION, a VALUE or an ENUM. On a COLOR, `auto` 1 keeps `value` as the colour to set again by itself (section 5.3.5) |
| 0x0024 | FEATURE_OPTIONS | `u8 id` (ENUM only) | `u8 count, char[24] option[count]` |
| 0x0099 | ACTION_STATUS | `u8 id, u32 word` (revision 1.34) | `u8 tone, u8 len, char text[len]`: the line under ACTION `id`'s button for the status word `word`, worded by the game; `NOT_FOUND` for an id with no status readout, `UNSUPPORTED` for a game that words none, `NOT_INGAME` outside a game |
| 0x0025 | SWITCH_PATCH | none | `u16 n, u16 pad, u32 stamp, { u32 addr, u32 value }[n], u16 nb, u16 pad, { u32 addr, u8 value, u8 pad[3] }[nb]`: the running game's code switches as the words and bytes an RPCS3 patch writes (revision 1.17, section 5.12.2). `UNSUPPORTED` from the PS3 module (revision 1.18) |

```
Feature (48 bytes):
u8   id
u8   kind
u8   group          index into DESCRIBE groups
u8   aux            ENUM: option count. SHADE COLOR (revision 1.24): the id of the COLOR it is worked out from. 0 for every other row
u8   flags          bit0 AUTO (same as toggle_auto), bit1 WRITES_CODE (instruction patch), bit2 SAVE_ASIDE (this ACTION asks the game to copy its current save into the savefile helper's aside buffer), bit3 LOAD_ASIDE (this ACTION asks the game to load what is in that buffer), bit4 LIVE (TOGGLE only, section 5.3.1), bit5 SIGNED (VALUE only, section 5.3.2), bit6 SHADE (COLOR only, section 5.3.3, revision 1.24), bit7 STOCK (COLOR only, section 5.3.4, revision 1.24)
u8   readout        VALUE, ENUM, COLOR: index into SessionInfo.readout[] that mirrors the current value, 0xFF if none. ACTION: 0xFF, or (revision 1.21) the index of its status readout, below. TOGGLE: 0xFF
u8   bits           VALUE: the width in bits of the field behind it, 8, 16 or 32. 0 means 32, so a row that names no width reads as it always did. 0 for every other kind (revision 1.7)
u8   flags2         revision 1.34, a pad byte before it: bit0 RUN_LEGAL, the row is run-legal (a client's and the Trainer's Manips page, never counted at a run start)
u32  min
u32  max            VALUE and ENUM range, inclusive; both 0 when unbounded. A SIGNED VALUE leaves both 0: `bits` already says what the range is
char label[32]
```

Toggle state travels in `toggle_state`; every other kind that has state travels through its readout, so a second client or a restarted one sees the real value.

**DESCRIBE's extension** (revision 1.34). After the features: `u8 ext_len`, then that many bytes, of which revision 1.34 sends 18: `u8 planet_flags` (PLANET_LOAD's bit0 and bit1 that the game's load acts on, section 5.5), `u8 menu_flags` (bit0 MENU_HERE: the Trainer can come up in this game on this platform: on a console in every game with a menu table, under RPCS3 only in ToD, whose Trainer is its pause menu), `char menu_row[16]` (the pause-menu row the Trainer takes, NUL-padded: Help, ToD's Inventory). A later revision may make it longer; a client reads what it knows. A module before revision 1.34 ends the reply with the features.

**An ACTION's status readout** (revision 1.21). An ACTION whose effect outlasts its answer may name a readout that says what it is doing and how it last ended, for a client to show beside its button. The word's layout is the action's own and is given where the action is described: UYA's Reset trophies (21, section 5.14) and, revision 1.29, Tools of Destruction's Cache cutscenes (19, section 5.17) name one. A client that does not know an action's layout ignores the readout, as every client before this revision does: they take a readout only from a VALUE, an ENUM or a COLOR. Since revision 1.34 a client needs no layout: ACTION_STATUS (0x0099) answers the line to draw for a word, in the game's words, and the reference client asks once a word and keeps no layout of its own.

#### 5.3.1 LIVE toggles (revision 1.3)

Most toggles are qwark's: an instruction patch, a freeze entry, a piece of module state. A few are not — they are a plain byte the game owns, which lives in the save file and which the player, another tool or the game itself can change at any moment. Those are flagged `LIVE`.

For a LIVE toggle:

- qwark re-reads the byte out of game memory every twelfth tick (10 Hz) while INGAME and sets `toggle_state` bit *id* to match. The client's checkbox therefore shows what the game says, not what qwark last wrote, and a client that connects mid-session sees the truth immediately.
- There is nothing for qwark to re-apply, so **FEATURE_SET_AUTO is answered UNSUPPORTED** and the bit never appears in `toggle_auto`. A client should draw no auto-apply control for a LIVE toggle.
- It never joins the previous-session record (section 4.1): after a reboot the byte comes back from the save file on its own.
- **FEATURE_SET still works** and still writes the byte, on and off. The next poll simply agrees with it.

The LIVE toggles today:

| Game | Feature id | Label | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| RaC1 | 6 | Goodies menu | one byte at 0x969CD3, part of the save |
| RaC1 | 24, 25, 26 | Update Ratchet / mobys / particles | three bits of the debug update word at 0x95C5C8 |
| RaC2 | 5 | Enable debug mode | one byte at 0x15B3070 |
| RaC3 | 5 | Quick-select pause | one byte at 0xC1E652 |

#### 5.3.2 Signed VALUEs (revision 1.7)

A VALUE is a `u32` on the wire, which is right for a bolt count and wrong for a field the game itself reads as signed: the QE offsets are halfwords whose useful value is -1, and a client that shows 65535 there is showing the bits rather than the number.

Two fields carry the width and the signedness:

- **`bits`**, the first of what used to be Feature's two pad bytes, is the width in bits of the field behind a VALUE: 8, 16 or 32. **0 means 32**, so every row written before this revision, and every row that has no reason to say, is read exactly as it was. Every other kind sends 0.
- **flags bit5 SIGNED** says that field is two's complement in `bits` bits. It only ever appears on a VALUE.

What each side does:

- The **readout** that mirrors the feature keeps carrying the **raw field**, zero-extended into the u32 as it always was: a QE offset of -1 arrives as 0x0000FFFF. The client sign-extends it from `bits` before showing it.
- **FEATURE_SET still carries `u32 value`**. For a signed feature the client sends the low `bits` bits of the number it wants, so -1 on a 16-bit field is 0xFFFF, and qwark writes the field exactly as it does for an unsigned one. Nothing about the write path changed.
- A signed row leaves **`min` and `max` both 0**, which already means unbounded. The width is the range: a client offers -32768..32767 on a 16-bit field and -2147483648..2147483647 on a 32-bit one, and clamps what the user types to it.

A client that predates this revision reads `bits` as padding and the flag as a bit it does not know, so it goes on showing the raw field: the same behaviour it had before, not a new failure.

The signed VALUEs today:

| Game | Feature id | Label | Field |
|---|---|---|---|
| RaC2 | 10 | Health XP | signed 32-bit |
| RaC2 | 23 | QE save write-offset | signed 16-bit halfword |
| RaC3 | 9 | Health XP | signed 32-bit |
| RaC3 | 14 | QE offset | signed 16-bit halfword |

#### 5.3.3 Shades (revision 1.24)

Some colours come in a set the game works out from one of them: Deadlocked's HUD draws in a team's colour and three variants of it. A COLOR row flagged **SHADE** (bit6) is one of the variants, and its `aux` names the COLOR it follows, which is listed before it.

- While a shade is **automatic**, the console works it out from the colour it follows, at every change of that colour. Its readout is the shade it worked out, with **bit 24 AUTO** (`0x01000000`) set.
- **FEATURE_SET with a value below `0x01000000`** overrides it: the shade is that colour, and a change of the colour it follows leaves it alone. Its readout carries no AUTO.
- **FEATURE_SET with bit 24 set** (`0x01000000`; the colour bits are ignored) makes it automatic again, worked out from the colour as it is now.

A client draws a shade as a COLOR that is not required: greyed out while it is automatic, so the user can see it is taken care of, and pickable all the same, a pick being an override, with a way back to automatic once it is overridden. A colour preset that leaves a shade out means the shade is automatic. Where the console keeps that state is the game's business: Deadlocked's shade is automatic while its word is 0 or exactly what the game's sums give, so an override that picks exactly the worked-out colour reads as automatic again, which it is in every way but name.

The shades today:

| Game | Feature id | Label | Follows | What it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Deadlocked | 19 | HUD vivid shade | 18, HUD colour | each channel times 1.5, capped at 255: the health bar, the ammo, the radar's cursor, the bot HUD |
| Deadlocked | 20 | HUD dark shade | 18 | each channel halved: the bottom corner of the HUD's gradients |
| Deadlocked | 21 | HUD light shade | 18 | each channel halfway to 255: the top corner of the prompt and pickup boxes, the radar cursor's pulse, the bot HUD's flash |

Deadlocked's sums are on the colour picked (section 5.3.4), and a shade of the game's own colour is the game's own shade: while the colour is stock an automatic shade is stock too. The brightness corrections come after, on whatever is handed out, and change no shade.

#### 5.3.4 Stock colours (revision 1.24)

A COLOR row flagged **STOCK** (bit7) has a colour of the game's own to go back to.

- Its readout carries **bit 24 AUTO** (`0x01000000`) while it is the game's own, and not once the user has picked a colour, or overridden one of the shades that follow it.
- **FEATURE_SET with bit 24 set** (the colour bits are ignored) puts it back to the game's own, and every shade that follows it to automatic.

A client draws a STOCK colour as any COLOR, with a way back to stock: since 2026-10-26 the reference client's colour presets offer "(none)", which sends AUTO to every STOCK colour of the block.

The STOCK colours today:

| Game | Feature id | Label | Stock |
|---|---|---|---|
| RaC2, UYA | 34, 35, 36 | Chargeboots primary front, back, tint | the words the game ships, alpha and all: `0x40FF8020`, `0x00CF6000`, `0x20FFFF00` (and UYA's tint control the word after it, `0x00008000`). Nothing in either game writes them, so a colour holds from one boot to the next |
| Deadlocked | 18 | HUD colour | the HUD team's four words in the game's team table, below |
| Deadlocked | 22, 23, 24 | Weapon colour V11, Weapon colour V99, Gadget colour | the table's team 0 colour `0x80734C00`, team 5 colour `0x80632773` and team 4 light word `0x8092CCDF` |

**Deadlocked's HUD colours are qwark's own code** (revision 1.24, 2026-10-26). The game keeps a team's colours in its team table (GuiMain_TeamColors, 0x9E87CC, eleven teams of four words `{ alpha, B, G, R }`: the colour, vivid, dark and light), and every part of the HUD that draws in the player's colour asks GuiMain_GetPlayerColor (0x360F78) for one, at build time or every frame: the health bar, the EXP bars, the ammo, the quick-select slots, the radar, the prompt and pickup boxes, the bot HUD, the end screen. It reads the word of the HUD's team: the HUD colour cheat's byte less one when it is on, the hero's team (1) otherwise, and the hero's team in multiplayer. qwark puts a trampoline at its last word, 0x36102C: for player one outside multiplayer it hands out qwark's word for the variant wherever that word is not 0, and the table's otherwise. The table is never written, so a HUD colour shares no word with a team the cheat can pick, nor with what names a team outright (the heal and hurt pulses, the vehicle's health, the menus' highlights, multiplayer, the cheat menu's swatches), none of which is part of the HUD's theme.

| Address | What |
|---|---|
| 0x15CD783 | the flat parts' correction, 1 or 0 (feature 25) |
| 0x15CD784 | the gradients' correction (feature 26) |
| 0x15CD788 | qwark's HUD words: colour, vivid, dark, light, `{ 0x80, B, G, R }` or 0 |
| 0x15CD798 | the weapons' words: V11, V99, gadget, the same |

All of it is BSS that no code of the game names, zero at every boot, so with nothing set the game draws exactly as it ships. Features 18 to 24 write the words and read them back: a readout is the colour picked, or where the word is 0 the table's word for the HUD's team (for 18 to 21) or the weapon's (22 to 24), flagged AUTO. The shades are automatic while their word is what GuiMain_InitTeamColors' sums give from the colour word (0 for 0, so a shade of the game's own colour is the game's own), and STOCK on 18 puts all four to 0.

**The brightness corrections.** The PS3 port draws an untextured quad twice as bright as its word says: the PS2 drew an untextured quad's colour as it stood and multiplied a texture by colour / 0x80, and the port draws every quad as a textured one, with a white stand-in for the untextured, so 0x80 is full brightness for all of them. The trampoline halves what it hands out, each channel rounded up and the alpha kept, while the right flag byte is 1: a call that draws a gradient (the health bar and its trail, the player EXP bar and the weapon EXP bar: seven calls, told apart by their return address) by 0x15CD784, feature 26 "Correct gradient brightness", and every other call by 0x15CD783, feature 25 "Correct HUD brightness". Both are TOGGLEs, off at every boot, with an auto flag like any. Off, the HUD draws as the PS3 version does, twice as bright; on, as the PS2 did, and so the colour picked. Neither changes a colour word or a readout. Under RPCS3 the toggles are read back out of their bytes while the code switches are in (section 5.12.2).

**Deadlocked's weapon colours** (22 to 24). The HUD draws a weapon's icon in the colour GuiMain_GetGadgetColor (0x3610F0) works out from three words of the team table: grey below V11 (a constant), from team 0's colour at V11 to team 5's at V99, team 0's at every level in multiplayer, and team 4's light word for a gadget that is no weapon. qwark's trampolines at its three loads (0x3611AC, 0x3611D0, 0x361224) take qwark's weapon words instead wherever they are not 0, so the weapons share no word with any team the HUD colour cheat picks, and a HUD colour never changes a weapon's, whatever team the cheat is on. An icon is a textured quad, which the port draws as the PS2 did, so no correction touches them. A set of 22 to 24 raises the HUD's weapon-icon bits (HudAmmo's dirty word, `0x01191BE0` bits 2 and 3), so the icons take the colours at the next frame.

The HUD copies some colours into its widgets when it is built: the player and weapon EXP bars (dark and colour), the prompt and pickup boxes (light, colour and dark, at half alpha), the quick-select slots and the radar's outline and fill (the colour). A FEATURE_SET on 18 to 21, 25 or 26 writes those copies as the trampoline would hand them out, keeping each copy's alpha, while a level is playing or paused, so they change with the parts that ask every frame. Only player one's HUD (the first canvas) is written.

**Where the code comes from.** On a console qwark writes the trampolines (one cave, 0x667DEC..0x667F04, in the dead lobby code the code switches use) and then the four branches as the session enters the game, once a process, and never takes them out, as it never takes RaC2's crash guards out (section 5.12.2): it writes nothing at all when a site holds anything but the game's word or its own branch. Under RPCS3 they are rows of the code switches' patch, with no flag byte of their own, so features 18 to 26 are flagged **WRITES_CODE** and need the patch and a restart there. The words are data on both platforms, written the same way.

#### 5.3.5 Colours kept for the boot (revision 1.24)

A COLOR's colour is game memory and goes with the game, so a client that wants it back at the next boot asks the console to keep it: **FEATURE_SET_AUTO** `u8 id, u8 1, u32 value`, `value` being what FEATURE_SET would carry (AUTO included: a shade kept automatic, a STOCK colour kept stock). The console stores it in config as the feature's own auto key, `<TITLEID>.auto.<slug>`, as bit 31 with the value in the low 25 bits (decimal, as every number config writes), so it is per user with settings profiles on (section 5.10.1), and sets the row's bit in `toggle_auto` and its DESCRIBE flags bit0. `auto` 0 forgets it. Nothing is read back from memory: a client keeps the value it shows, and sends FEATURE_SET_AUTO again when it changes a colour it has kept (the reference client does at the end of a pick, after a preset, after "(none)").

When the console sets them, by FEATURE_SET in DESCRIBE order, so a colour goes in before its shades:

| Game | When |
|---|---|
| RaC2, UYA | as the session enters INGAME, the chargeboot words being static data nothing in the game writes |
| Deadlocked | as the session enters the game, after qwark's HUD code is in on a console, and under RPCS3 once the look finds the code switches (the HUD's COLORs are WRITES_CODE there, and wait as the toggles do). The words are qwark's own, which nothing in the game writes, so once a process is enough |

A kept toggle is still applied at INGAME as before; Deadlocked's two brightness toggles are flag bytes, set at once.

### 5.4 Memory (0x003x)

All of these except the LIST ops return NOT_INGAME outside INGAME. Watches and freezes carry values right-aligned in a u64.

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0030 | MEM_READ | `u32 addr, u32 len` (len at most 16384 since revision 1.11, 65536 before it) | `len` bytes |
| 0x0031 | MEM_WRITE | `u32 addr, bytes` (at most 16380 bytes of data: the frame is 16384 and the address is four of them) | none |
| 0x0032 | WATCH_ADD | `u32 addr, u8 size` (1, 2, 4 or 8) | `u8 id`. Adding the same address and size again returns the existing id |
| 0x0033 | WATCH_REMOVE | `u8 id` | none |
| 0x0034 | WATCH_LIST | none | `u8 n, { u8 id, u8 size, u8 pad[2], u32 addr }[n]` |
| 0x0035 | FREEZE_ADD | `u32 addr, u8 size, u8 pad[3], u64 value` | `u8 id` |
| 0x0036 | FREEZE_REMOVE | `u8 id` | none |
| 0x0037 | FREEZE_LIST | none | `u8 n, { u8 id, u8 size, u8 pad[2], u32 addr, u64 value }[n]` |
| 0x0038 | PATCH_APPLY | `u16 nwords, u16 pad, { u32 addr, u32 word }[nwords]` (at most 64 words) | none. A client patch keyed by its first address; applying it twice is a no-op. Since revision 1.11 the sixteen client-patch slots share a pool of 256 words, so a patch that would run the pool over is answered `FULL`, the same status a client already gets when every slot is taken |
| 0x0039 | PATCH_REVERT | `u32 first_addr` | none |
| 0x003A | PATCH_LIST | none | `u8 n, { u32 first_addr, u16 nwords, u8 kind, u8 pad, char[32] name }[n]`. kind: 0 client, 1 feature, 2 mod |
| 0x003B | CLEAR_CLIENT | none | none. Removes every watch, freeze and client patch. Toggles and mods are untouched |

### 5.5 Positions and planets (0x004x)

Eight slots per planet per game. Slot contents are opaque game-defined blobs of at most 64 bytes.

POS_SELECT and PLANET_SELECT write straight through to `config.txt`, so the selection is remembered across a client restart and across a console reboot; no CONFIG_SAVE is needed. It comes back in `SessionInfo.selected_slot`, and in `selected_planet` and `planet_flags`.

**Whose planet selection it is (build 53).** The slot is one for the console. The planet and its flags are one game's, the way the manip options are (section 5.14): a planet id means something only inside its own game, and so do the resets beside it. PLANET_SELECT sets the running game's, SessionInfo reports the running game's, and a PLANET_LOAD of 0xFF, which is what the load-planet combo sends, takes the running game's, so a box ticked in one game is not ticked in the next. Outside a game - at the XMB, while the next game boots, and in a title no game table claims (section 3.3) - the selection is the last game's that was INGAME: that is the game a client goes on showing, greyed, until another arrives, so its boxes neither empty at the XMB nor fill again when it comes back. The first block that says a new game is INGAME is the first that carries its selection; a BOOTING block whose `game` already names a candidate still carries the last game's. Before any game has run since the module loaded there is no game to keep one for: both fields are 0 and PLANET_SELECT answers `UNSUPPORTED`. A game that has never had a selection of its own reads the console-wide pair from before build 53 (section 5.10), and 0 when there is none.

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0040 | POS_SELECT | `u8 slot` | none |
| 0x0041 | POS_SAVE | `u8 slot`, 0xFF = selected | none |
| 0x0042 | POS_LOAD | `u8 slot`, 0xFF = selected | none |
| 0x0043 | POS_LIST | none | `u8 planet, u8 nslots, { u8 filled, u8 pad[3], f32 x, f32 y, f32 z }[nslots]` for the current planet |
| 0x0044 | POS_CLEAR | `u8 slot` | none |
| 0x0045 | PLANET_LIST | none | `u8 count, char[24] name[count], u8 order[count]`; index is the game's planet index. `order` since build 53, see below |
| 0x0046 | PLANET_SELECT | `u8 planet, u8 flags` (bit0 reset level flags, bit1 reset special bolts; which a game acts on, see below) | none. Build 53: the running game's selection, or the last game's; `UNSUPPORTED` before any game has run (above) |
| 0x0047 | PLANET_LOAD | `u8 planet` 0xFF = selected, `u8 flags` 0xFF = selected, the running game's selection since build 53 | none. The planet request goes out first, then the flags act on the planet being loaded. Build 53: RaC2 under RPCS3 without the code switches' patch answers `UNSUPPORTED` for a trip its crash guards are for (section 5.12.2) |
| 0x0048 | DIE | none | none |
| 0x0049 | MOBY_TABLE | none | `u32 table_ptr_addr, u32 table_end_ptr_addr, u16 stride, u16 flags`, and with flags bit1 `u16 live_offset, u16 dead_value`. The client reads the pointers and the entries with MEM_READ. Revision 1.34: flags (a pad before, 0 for a flat table) bit0 LAST_INDEX, the end word is the last index in use; bit1 LIVE_TEST, a slot whose u16 at `live_offset` is `dead_value` is free (Tools of Destruction; the revision's paragraph) |
| 0x004A | POS_EDIT | `u8 slot, u8 pad[3], f32 x, f32 y, f32 z` | none. Revision 1.13 |
| 0x004B | POS_STORE | `u8 slot, u8 len, u8 pad[2], bytes[len]` | none. Revision 1.13 |

**The menu order (revision 1.21, build 53).** After the names, PLANET_LIST carries `count` more bytes: the planet indices in the order a menu should list them, every index from 0 to `count - 1` exactly once. It is the order the game visits its planets rather than the order it numbers them, and it changes nothing else: a name is still at its id, every request and every telemetry field still carries the id, and the autosplit route lines are still in id order. UYA's order is the old client's combo box (Veldin, Florana, Starship Phoenix, Marcadia, Daxx, Obani Gemini, Blackwater City, Annihilation Nation, Aquatos and its two mission levels, and so on, the vid comics last by their own number), and Deadlocked's is its combo box too, which is the numbering but for Orxon before Valix; RaC1's and RaC2's numbering is their order, and the bytes are 0, 1, 2 and on. The placeholder names, `(none)`, `(unused)` and `(infinite loop)`, come after every real planet. Tools of Destruction's names (revision 1.29) are the planets and places the ship's planet selector shows for its levels, Kerwan for Metropolis, Kortog for Stratus City and so on, Fastoon II for the return to Fastoon (the revision's paragraph above has the list), in LEVEL_ order, which is also its menu order. A module before build 53 sends the names alone, and a client that finds no bytes after them, or bytes that are not every index once, lists the planets in id order.

**Deadlocked: a locked planet is unlocked first (build 54).** Deadlocked crashes when it arrives on a planet the save has not unlocked: with no mission of the planet's unlocked or visible, it drops the player onto an empty Missions screen, and any press there brings the game down. So a Deadlocked PLANET_LOAD of a planet the save has not unlocked (bit 1 of its level save's status byte clear) first leaves the save as the game leaves it when that planet unlocks the long way, and only then sends the request: the console menu's planet picker and its Planets page load through the same code (section 9.1). From the game's own progress code (the PS2 prototype's `MF_UpdateLevelProgress`, `MF_UpdateMissionProgress`, `LevelBox::CheckUnlockLevel` and `CheckUnlockMission`; the PS3 code at 0x1D62E0 is the same), the unlock writes **two bytes of the save**, in the planet's `MF_LevelSave` (0xB27FF0 + (planet - 1) x 0x304):
- its **first mission's status** (+0x08, `mission[0].status`): bits 0x01 visible and 0x02 unlocked or'd in;
- then **the level's status** (+0x300): bits 0x01 visible and 0x02 unlocked or'd in.

Nothing else: no badges, Dread points, armour, completions, and not the next-mission word. That is what the game's rules leave in a planet that has just unlocked: every planet's first mission is a main mission that asks for nothing (the PS3 executable's MissionDefs at 0x9E06A4), and the arrival's own progress pass, which runs before the level picks its mission, makes the planet's other missions visible and unlocks those whose own rules allow, so the player arrives in the planet's first mission. A planet the save has unlocked already, DreadZone (which asks for nothing) and the placeholders are left as they are. The bytes are save data: **the next save or autosave keeps the unlock**, and nothing in the game ever locks a planet again. Build 54's Unlock all planets (feature 11, retired in build 59) did the same for every planet it leaves locked, Interior included, and sends its load of DreadZone last, once everything is written.

**Deadlocked, 1.4.1: where the unlock goes, and co-op.** The unlock is save data, so it goes only into a save file's game held still: out of the front end (the byte at 0xB3C7D4 clear), in a single-player or co-op level (the level at 0x9C3240 from 1 to 40), not multiplayer (g_gameType at 0xB36DF4 is 0), and in ordinary play or the pause with no mode change queued (gameMode 0 or 3 at 0xB3C5A0 and pendingGameMode -2 at 0x9C37F4, so not in a load). Anywhere else a PLANET_LOAD of a planet the save has not unlocked is `BUSY`, with nothing written; a planet it has unlocked, DreadZone and the placeholders load as before, wherever the game is. Unlock all planets, all of it save data, is `BUSY` there the same way. The order is the fast-load force first, so a force that fails leaves the save as it was, then the unlock, then the level and the request word, last. In co-op (g_gameType 0 and g_gameRules 1 at 0xB36DF8) the level written is the planet's id plus 20, as the game's own travel maps it (SwitchToLevel, 0x18DA24), so a co-op load brings up co-op's level: until 1.4.1 every Deadlocked load, the console menu's too, brought up the single-player level with co-op's rules on. The ids on the wire stay 1 to 15, and the unlock goes into the planet's own level save, which co-op shares (MF_LevelSave[(level - 1) % 20]); Unlock all planets' load is co-op's DreadZone, 21. Co-op has not yet been run on a console.

**What the planet flags do in each game.** PLANET_LOAD sends the request first and only then acts on the flags, on the planet being loaded, and only when the request went out (ToD's, since build 59, acts on them just before its request, once every check has passed: the load reads the destination's flags to pick its fade). Since revision 1.34 DESCRIBE's extension says which bits a game's load acts on. bit0 zeroes the destination's level flags in RaC1, RaC2 and UYA, and since build 59 ToD's (that planet's group of its one table, as LEVELFLAGS_RESET clears it). bit1 takes back the destination's special bolts: RaC1's four gold bolts, since build 59 RaC2's platinum bolts (four bytes a level from 0x1562540; the game's own count gives level 26's four to Maktar, 2, so a load of Maktar clears both and one of 26 neither) and ToD's gold bolts (the level's mask in its level save), and since build 53 UYA's titanium bolts, every one collected on that planet. UYA keeps them eight bytes to a planet id from 0xECE54D, and a bolt belongs to the level it is found in: the five vid comic bolts the game keeps in Starship Phoenix's eight bytes are each taken back by a load of their comic, and a Phoenix load takes back the Phoenix's own two. Before build 53 UYA's load ignored bit1, so a client offers that box for UYA against build 53 or later only. Deadlocked has neither and ignores both bits. Both are data writes and work under RPCS3 as on a console (section 3.2).

**Writing a slot the player is not standing in (revision 1.13).** POS_SAVE captures a blob from the live game and is the only way a slot was ever filled. POS_EDIT and POS_STORE are the other two a client needs: POS_EDIT for coordinates the user typed into a box, POS_STORE for a blob the client already has. Both act on the current planet, the one POS_LIST reports, and both persist the moment they succeed exactly as POS_SAVE does; no CONFIG_SAVE is needed. Both need the session to be INGAME and answer **NOT_INGAME** otherwise, and both answer **UNSUPPORTED** where the running game has no position slots. A slot outside 0..7 is **BAD_ARG**.

POS_EDIT replaces only the three coordinates of a slot that is already filled. The rest of the blob - the rotation, and the camera floats Deadlocked keeps - is left exactly as the game wrote it, so a position nudged sideways still comes back facing the way it was captured. It is the game that decides which bytes of its blob are x, y and z, the same knowledge POS_LIST's reply is built from, so the coordinates POS_EDIT writes are the ones the next POS_LIST reports and the ones the next POS_LOAD puts in the game. An empty slot is **NOT_FOUND**: there is no blob to preserve, and a client that wants to fill an empty slot from nothing uses POS_STORE.

POS_STORE replaces the whole blob of a slot and marks it filled, whether or not it was filled before. `len` must equal the running game's blob length to the byte (or, since revision 1.31, an earlier length that game's POS_LOAD still reads: Tools of Destruction's 48 bytes beside its 64), and anything else is **BAD_ARG**: a blob is opaque and self-describing only to the game that made it, so one of the wrong shape - another game's, or one saved before that game's blob changed - would be stored happily and then misread by POS_LOAD. The lengths themselves are not on the wire and never have been: a blob is only ever handed back to the game it came from, so nothing needed to name its size. A client importing old slots therefore keeps the lengths beside its own game knowledge and checks the running game first; a blob it cannot place is one to keep rather than to store, and the BAD_ARG is the console refusing to take its word for it.

**Tools of Destruction's slots (2026-11-13).** A ToD blob is 64 bytes: the hero's position, collision centre and its last value (48 bytes from the hero's +0x1260, as before), then the camera's offset, camera position less the point it looks at (a vec4). Since 2026-11-14 the camera is the slot a camera client of `g_camClients` (0x101CC2E0, four `ToDCamClient`s of 0x21D0 bytes) is using, `active` at +0x2114, else the first slot of either type in any client, and it is either the follow camera (type 3, `ToDFollowCam`: the offset is its +0xD0 as it is) or, with Game Options' Lock-Strafe on (`g_saveData.lockStrafe`, 0x1021D32C, 1), the lock-strafe camera (type 26, `ToDStrafeCam`: its +0xD0 is the unit direction it looks in, and the offset is that direction turned back and 5.5 units long, where `StrafeCam_place` puts the camera). Until then only client 0's first slot of type 3 was looked for, so with Lock-Strafe on no slot ever carried the camera. A slot saved with either camera loads into either: into the lock-strafe camera POS_LOAD writes the offset turned back and made unit long as its direction, its target moved by the hero's jump and its position the target plus the offset. POS_LOAD writes the hero back as before, then the offset as both the camera's offset and its desired one, so the game's next frame keeps it (the yaw holds, the pitch eases back to the camera's own as it always does), and moves the camera's target and positions by the hero's own jump, so the camera does not swing in from the old place. A camera whose target is not within 32 units of the hero is not the hero's, and is neither saved nor written; a slot saved then, and every slot saved before this change, is 48 bytes and loads the hero alone. POS_EDIT changes the three coordinates and keeps the camera. POS_STORE takes 64 bytes or 48, a slot without the camera, stored as it is and loaded as the hero alone, so an older ToD slot a client kept can be put back; any other length is `BAD_ARG`. An offset that is not finite, or shorter than 0.25 or longer than 64 units, is left out at load.

**Tools of Destruction's PLANET_LOAD outside play (2026-11-14).** In play (`g_gameState` 4) PLANET_LOAD writes `g_planetLoadRequest`, which the game's next frames fade out and load, as `LoadLevel` does in play. In the pause (5) and during a realtime cinematic (9) no gameplay frame runs to take that request, and until 2026-11-14 the load was `BUSY` there. Now it is what `LoadLevel` itself does there, and what the pause menu's own Load (Options > Load) does: the level's name set as `Level_setName` sets it (`g_levelNameLastSet` 0x10387D18 and `g_levelName` 0x10387D20 the name from `Level_getName`'s table at 0x1006E6BC, `g_prevLevelName` 0x10387D1C the old one), `g_currentLevel`, then `GameState_Request(3, 3)`: the top three tiers of `g_tierStack` (0x1073B3B8) marked to go (their byte +4) and `g_requestedGameState` (0x100FA21C) 3, written last. The game's next frame unloads the level, and the level tier's unload (`LevelTier_closePauseAndMovies`, 0x3C0208) runs `PauseMenu_exit` and `Movie_stopAll`, the pause's and the cinematic's own exits, so either is taken down as the game takes it down; there is no fade-out. Fast loads are forced for it as for every qwark load, and the autosplitter sees the tier loader on the level's tiers as for any load. Still `BUSY`, with nothing written: the galactic map (6) and an FMV (7), whose own exits (`GalMapState_exit`, `FmvState_exit`) the unload does not run; a pause whose loading thread is still building it (`g_pauseMenuState` 1: `PauseMenu_exit` would free the menu under it); a planet load or a state change already asked for; no level up. With the Trainer's glue in the pause (`g_pauseMenuState` 3), a client's PLANET_LOAD is still the menu's (revision 1.29: the pause closed by the glue, then the load in play).

**Tools of Destruction's DIE (2026-11-13).** The game's own respawn, without the death animation: qwark puts the screen fade at black (`ScreenFade_Set(1.0)`'s fields in the level object) and `g_heroRespawnFadeState` (0x101F1E70) at 1, which is where a death's `Hero_beginRespawnFade` leaves them, and the game's next gameplay frame (`Hero_respawnWhenBlack`) asks for the level tier's respawn itself: the hero back at the last checkpoint with full hit points, a new lua_State and the level's fade in from black, and no load. Only in gameplay with no state change, planet load or cinematic under way, `BUSY` otherwise; from the game's pause menu (the Trainer's Game page) it answers OK and is made as the pause closes. The hit points are not written. The death's difficulty count is not made.

### 5.6 Unlocks and level flags (0x005x)

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0050 | UNLOCK_LIST | none | `u8 ncat, char[24] category[ncat], UnlockFieldDesc[4], u8 n, Unlock[n]`, values read live |
| 0x0051 | UNLOCK_SET | `u8 id, u8 field, u16 pad, u32 value` | none. A field the entry does not declare is UNSUPPORTED |
| 0x0052 | reserved | | |
| 0x0053 | LEVELFLAGS_GET | `u8 planet` | `u16 len, bytes`, the game's flag regions for that planet concatenated |
| 0x0054 | LEVELFLAGS_RESET | `u8 planet` | none. The same reset PLANET_LOAD's bit0 performs |
| 0x0055 | LEVELFLAGS_SET | `u8 planet, u8 value, u16 offset` | none. Writes one byte at `offset` into the concatenated region returned by LEVELFLAGS_GET; an offset past its end is BAD_ARG |
| 0x0056 | LEVELFLAGS_BIT | `u8 planet, u8 on, u16 bit` | none. Revision 1.28. Sets (`on` non-zero) or clears bit `bit & 7` of byte `bit >> 3` of that region, read and written on the console; a byte past its end is BAD_ARG, a bit already as asked is OK and writes nothing |
| 0x0057 | LEVELFLAGS_NAMES | | Retired in revision 1.29, never reused: `UNKNOWN_OP`. It was revision 1.28's names of a game's flag bits (`u8 ngroups, LevelFlagGroup[ngroups], u16 n, LevelFlagName[n]`), which only Tools of Destruction answered; its names are the client's now (`data/levelflags/tod.json` in RaCMAN Reloaded) |

```
UnlockFieldDesc (16 bytes), revision 1.3, one per value slot 0..3, always four:
char name[12]        NUL-padded, e.g. "Owned", "Gold", "Level", "XP", "Ammo".
                     Empty = the game never uses this slot; the client draws
                     no column for it and no row declares it
u8   kind            0 flag (draw a checkbox, write 0 or 1)
                     1 number (draw a number box)
u8   max             kind 1: the largest meaningful value, 0 = no limit
u8   reserved[2]     zero

Unlock (44 bytes):
u8   id
u8   category
u8   fields          bit f set = value slot f is meaningful for this entry
u8   pad
u32  value[4]        one per slot, as the UnlockFieldDesc rows describe them
char name[24]
```

Before revision 1.3 the four slots were fixed as owned / gold / level / ammo, which was RaC1's reading of them and wrong everywhere else. The `UnlockFieldDesc` rows are now the authority; slot 0 is "Owned" in every game qwark knows, but nothing on the wire requires that.

What the four games ship today:

| Game | Slot 0 | Slot 1 | Slot 2 | Slot 3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RaC1 | Owned, flag | Gold, flag | — | Ammo, number |
| RaC2 | Owned, flag | **Level, number, max 4** | — | Ammo, number |
| RaC3 | Owned, flag | **Level, number, max 8** | **XP, number** | Ammo, number |
| Deadlocked | Owned, flag | **Level, number, max 99** | **Ammo, number** | — |

RaC2's slots are RaC3's bar the XP, because the two games keep the same item system: every item, gadget and weapon version has an item id, and the game keeps an owned byte, an ammunition word, an experience word and a version byte per id. Slot 1 is that version, and what crosses the wire is the step in the weapon's own chain — v1, v2, v3, v4 — not the item id the game records it with: a V2 Lancer holds the Lancer V2's item id, and UNLOCK_SET is what turns a level into it. `max` is 4, the game-wide maximum; sixteen weapons reach V4, the rest stop at V2, and UNLOCK_SET clamps to the entry's own count rather than refusing. A weapon whose version byte names no version of itself reads level **0**, a number the field never otherwise takes. Slot 3 is per BASE weapon: a V4 Lancer's rounds are still the Lancer's own word, and only the magazine size "Max all weapon ammo" fills to comes from the V4's stats. Only two weapons have no second version — the Zodiac and the RYNO II — and declare no level; the Clank Zapper and the five RaC1 carry-overs (Tesla Claw, Bomb Glove, Walloper, Visibomb Gun and Decoy Glove, whose second and last version is bought from Slim Cognito rather than earned) have two, and everything else four. RaC2's gadgets and items are owned or not owned and declare slot 0 alone. Ticking Owned leaves the version byte where it is, so a weapon taken away and handed back comes back on the version it had.

RaC2's slot 2 is unnamed. Build 33 offered it as an XP number — the raw experience word the game keeps, thirty-two times the figure the weapon's HUD bar counts — and build 34 withdrew it, because the column did not fit the table a client draws. The word is still in the game and qwark simply no longer reads or writes it: no row declares the slot, UNLOCK_LIST reports 0 there and UNLOCK_SET on it answers UNSUPPORTED. Ammo stayed in slot 3 rather than moving down, because slot numbers are part of this contract and a client draws only the named ones.

RaC3's slot 1 is the weapon version UYAUnlocks drew as a v1..v8 combo box, not a gold flag: gold weapons are a RaC1 idea and RaC3 has none. `max` is the game-wide maximum, so a client may offer 8 for every weapon; UNLOCK_SET clamps to the entry's own level count, and the R3YNO — the one weapon that stops at v5 — lands on v5. Writing 1 is a real downgrade, not "unset".

Deadlocked's slot 1 is the weapon version too, and its slot 2 is that weapon's ammunition rather than any experience the game keeps. The level that crosses the wire is the one the game shows, V1..V99, and a client needs to know nothing else: 1 is the lowest version a weapon can have, and a weapon the player does not have reads Owned 0 and level **0**, a number the field never otherwise takes. `max` is 99, the version a weapon reaches in challenge mode; a first playthrough stops at V10, and nothing in the entry says which mode the file is in. Unlike RaC3's version, that 99 is a hard limit rather than a clamp — the game misbehaves above V99, so UNLOCK_SET answers BAD_ARG for a larger level, and for 0, and writes nothing either time. Its bot upgrades are one owned byte each and declare slot 0 alone, so the level and ammo columns belong to the weapons.

The table has three categories, in this order: Weapons, then Gadgets, then Bot upgrades. Gadgets is the two pairs of boots, the Charge Boots and the Gravity Boots, which sit in the same gadget table as the weapons and are taken and given by the same halfword, but have no version and no ammunition of their own, so those two rows declare slot 0 alone the way a bot upgrade does.

Setting field 0 to 1 may carry its game's side effects: in RaC1 a weapon is handed its maximum ammo, as the old client did. In Deadlocked owning a locked weapon hands it V1, and a weapon that already has a version keeps the one it has, so the checkbox never undoes a level. Clearing it takes the weapon away again and leaves the ammunition where it was.

Deadlocked's feature id 16, "Reset all weapon levels", is retired and never reused, so DESCRIBE no longer lists it. "Max all weapon levels" (15) and "Max all weapon ammo" (17) keep their ids, and both pass over a weapon the player does not have rather than handing it out, and over the two gadget rows entirely. RaC2 has the same pair under ids 38 and 39, and RaC3's "Max all weapon levels" is id 26; the labels are word for word the same in all three, so a client can lay them out together. Revision 1.34: Tools of Destruction has all three, 27 to 29, with the same labels, and RaC1, whose upgrade is the gold weapon, "Upgrade all weapons to gold" (29) and "Reset all gold weapons" (30); every game's pass over a weapon the player does not have (UYA's since that revision).

An entry may withhold a slot its category otherwise offers, and UNLOCK_SET on that slot is then UNSUPPORTED: RaC3's Suck Cannon declares no ammo, because the game does not count any for it, and RaC2's Zodiac and RYNO II declare no level.

A whole category may withhold one. The four descriptor rows say what a game names, not what every row carries, so a client draws a column from the descriptors and then a cell only where the row's `fields` bit is set. In RaC3 the level, XP and ammo slots belong to the weapons: its gadgets, items and vid comics are owned or not owned and declare slot 0 alone.

An id is retired rather than renumbered when an entry goes: the remaining rows keep the ids they had, the list simply gets shorter, and UNLOCK_SET on the retired id answers BAD_ARG. RaC3's id 0, the Bomb Glove, is the one retired so far, dropped because UYA has no way to reach the item in game.

```
LevelFlagGroup (24 bytes), revision 1.28:
u8   planet          the PLANET_LIST id the group is that planet's flags,
                     0xFF for a group that is no planet's
char name[23]        NUL-padded

LevelFlagName (6 + len bytes):
u16  bit             the flag's place in LEVELFLAGS_GET's region: byte bit >> 3,
                     mask 1 << (bit & 7), the numbering LEVELFLAGS_BIT takes
u16  number          the game's own number for the flag
u8   group           index into the groups above
u8   len
char name[len]       not NUL-terminated
```

(LevelFlagGroup and LevelFlagName are what LEVELFLAGS_NAMES sent until revision 1.29 retired it; they are kept here for a client that still parses them.) The flags come grouped, the groups in their own order and the flags of a group together, in the order a client lists them. LEVELFLAGS_NAMES was static: it does not depend on the save, and a client asks it once a session and reads the values with LEVELFLAGS_GET.

**Tools of Destruction** (revision 1.28) keeps one table of progression flags for the whole save rather than a region per planet: the game's GLOBAL_FLAG_ enum, flags 0 to 291, one bit each in five big-endian u64 words at `g_saveData`+0x5528 (0x10211398). Flag n is bit n & 63 of word n >> 6, the way the level scripts' check_flag, set_flag and clear_flag test it, so on the wire it is byte (n >> 6) * 8 + 7 - ((n & 63) >> 3), bit n & 7. LEVELFLAGS_GET answers those 40 bytes whatever the planet (any PLANET_LIST id; 19 and up is BAD_ARG), and LEVELFLAGS_SET writes one of them. LEVELFLAGS_RESET clears the flags grouped under that planet and leaves every other bit, writing only the bytes that change. Since revision 1.29 the names are the client's (`data/levelflags/tod.json`) and qwark keeps only each flag's group, by number, for the reset; the client's file has the same groups. They are 20 groups, the 19 planets in LEVEL_ order and then "Other" (0xFF) for the hero's hints, the RYNO's messages and the items, and all 292 flags, named as the old RaCMAN named them, which are the game's own Lua constants; the 34 the old list left out, 0 to 33, have the game's names. PLANET_LOAD's bit0 does nothing in ToD: the game never reset these, and the old client did not either.

A game that does not offer level flags answers all three ops UNSUPPORTED, and a client that gets that is expected to hide its level-flag panel for that game. RaC2 and RaC3 return 0x10 bytes per planet. RaC1 and Deadlocked answer UNSUPPORTED: Deadlocked has no such region, and RaC1's is not laid out the way the old client's viewer assumed, so it is withheld until the format has been worked out. RaC1's PLANET_LOAD bit0 still resets the flags, which is the one thing racman actually did with them.

### 5.7 Mods (0x006x)

Mods live under `/dev_hdd0/qwark/mods/<TITLEID>/<dirname>/` on the console. The client owns the library and uploads a mod the first time it is used or whenever its content changes: it computes CRC32 over `patch.txt` followed by every referenced `.bin` in patch order, compares it to the `hash` reported by MOD_LIST, and if they differ writes the files with the file ops, writes `qwark.sum` containing the eight hex digits, then MOD_RESCAN and MOD_LOAD.

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0060 | MOD_LIST | none | `u8 n, Mod[n]` |
| 0x0061 | MOD_LOAD | `char[32] dirname` | none |
| 0x0062 | MOD_UNLOAD | `char[32] dirname` | none |
| 0x0063 | MOD_SET_AUTO | `char[32] dirname, u8 auto` | none. Persisted in config; build 56: FULL when config.txt has no room for it, the flag then as it was |
| 0x0064 | MOD_RESCAN | none | none |
| 0x0065 | MOD_INFO | `char[32] dirname` | description text, at most 1024 bytes |
| 0x0066 | MOD_PATCH | `u8 index, u8 pad, u16 page` (the Mod row's `index`; a request before revision 1.19 is `u8 index, u8 pad[3]`, page 0) | One page: `u16 n, u16 pages, u32 stamp, { u32 addr, u32 value }[n], u16 nb, u16 pad, { u32 addr, u8 value, u8 pad[3] }[nb]`: the mod as the words and bytes an RPCS3 patch writes (revision 1.16), a page at a time (revision 1.19), section 5.7.1. `UNSUPPORTED` from the PS3 module (revision 1.18) |
| 0x0067 | MOD_USB_LIST | none | `u8 n, UsbMod[n]`: the mods on the console's USB drives (revision 1.24, section 5.7.2). `UNSUPPORTED` where code cannot be patched |

```
Mod (120 bytes):
u8   index
u8   flags           bit0 loaded, bit1 auto, bit2 needs_lua, bit3 previous, bit4 parse_error,
                     bit5 checking (revision 1.16), bit6 legal, bit7 illegal (revision 1.30)
u8   pad[2]
u32  hash            content of qwark.sum, 0 when absent
char dirname[32]
char name[32]
char version[16]
char author[32]
```

**MOD_RESCAN** reads every mod folder of the current title again, and that is what makes the upload above land: the `qwark.sum` just written becomes the `hash` MOD_LIST reports, the `#-` lines become the name, version and author in the row, and the patch words become what the next MOD_LOAD writes into the game. It answers OK whatever it finds, including for a title with no mod folder at all. Since revision 1.24 it also reads the console's USB drives again (section 5.7.2), and a mod that is still there keeps its `index`: a rescan used to number every mod that was not loaded afresh, in the order the folder listing gave them, so a folder added or removed could move another mod's bit in SessionInfo's `mod_loaded`, `mod_auto` and `mod_previous`. A new folder takes the lowest index free, and the index of a folder that has gone is free again.

A mod that is loaded at that moment is the one exception, and it is left exactly as it was parsed: its patch words are in the running game next to the originals its unload will put back, so re-reading its folder would leave the console unable to give the game its own instructions again. Its row therefore keeps the hash it was parsed with, and a client that uploads over a mod the console has loaded goes on seeing the old hash until it unloads the mod and rescans. Every other mod is re-read whatever is loaded, so a client that uploads a mod in order to load it — which is the only time the mods panel uploads one — always sees what it just wrote. A rescan used to skip *every* folder it already knew as soon as any one mod was loaded, so an uploaded mod kept the hash and the words of the copy it replaced for the rest of the session; that is fixed, and no client has to do anything differently for it.

Every build from build 42 to build 57 had a second fault in the same place, fixed after build 57 with no change on the wire. A rescan packs the words of the mods it keeps loaded down to the front of qwark's word pool, and it packed them in the order of their indices, as if their words lay in the pool in that order. They do not once a mod is loaded after one with a higher index: the higher one's words were packed first by the rescan after its own load, and the lower one's were read in after them. The rescan after the second load then moved the lower mod's words over the higher one's before those had moved, and left the higher mod holding the lower one's words and the originals they replaced. Its unload (MOD_UNLOAD, or Off on the console menu's Mods page) put the other mod's words back, cleared its LOADED bit and left its own words in the game, and PATCH_LIST and the Mods page's guard (section 9.1) took the other mod's addresses for its own, so the page refused a mod that shares no byte with it as one that "would write over" it. On a console it was seen on Deadlocked: Stereo Music (index 10, auto-loaded) and IL Decimal Timer (index 4, loaded for IL RTA), after which PATCH_LIST named Stereo Music for 16 of IL Decimal Timer's words at 0x003D8858 and the page refused IL RTA over Stereo Music. A rescan now packs the words in the order they lie in the pool, and the caves the same way. A game in that state keeps it until it is restarted: what is in its memory is as qwark wrote it, and only qwark's record of it is wrong, so the cure is a restart of the game rather than any op.

**The flags.**

- bit0 **LOADED**: qwark has the mod in this process, by MOD_LOAD, an auto-apply at boot or a PREVIOUS_REAPPLY. On a platform that refuses code patches (SessionInfo `flags` bit2) it means instead that the mod is in game memory, every cave byte and every word of it, which is how a mod an RPCS3 patch put in at load shows up (revision 1.16, section 5.7.1).
- bit1 **AUTO**: the mod re-applies itself silently when the game boots (MOD_SET_AUTO).
- bit2 **NEEDS_LUA**: `patch.txt` names an `automation:` script, which qwark never runs. On a console the mod's words and caves still load.
- bit3 **PREVIOUS**: the mod's bit is set in the previous-session record (SessionInfo `mod_previous`, section 4.1) and the mod has not been loaded again since; it goes when the mod loads and when the record is re-applied or dismissed. Before build 49 this bit was documented and never set, so a client that read it read 0.
- bit4 **PARSE_ERROR**: `patch.txt` could not be read or does not fit qwark's tables. MOD_LOAD answers `IO_ERROR`.
- bit5 **CHECKING** (revision 1.16): only on a platform that refuses code patches, and only while INGAME: qwark has not yet looked for this mod in the running game, so bit0 says nothing yet. It is never set on a console.
- bit6 **LEGAL** and bit7 **ILLEGAL** (revision 1.30): the mod's `patch.txt` has a line `#- legal: true` (LEGAL) or `#- legal: false` (ILLEGAL), the key and the value in any case, the value trimmed; its author says whether it is allowed in a speedrun. Neither bit is a mod of **unknown** legality: no such line, an empty one, or any other value (`yes`, `1`, `maybe`); where a file has the line twice, the last one counts. Never both. A loaded mod keeps what it was parsed with across a rescan, as it keeps the rest of its row. The run-start warnings count the mods in the game with bit7 and with neither (section 5.10.2); a client shows them as it likes. An earlier module sends both bits 0, which reads as unknown, and is right: it never read the line.

### 5.7.1 Mods under RPCS3 (revision 1.16)

qwark cannot load a mod under RPCS3: a mod is patch words, code caves or both, and the emulator recompiles PPU code before it runs it, so words and caves written into a running game are memory and nothing more (section 3.2). RPCS3 applies its own patch files as it loads the executable, though, before it compiles anything, so a mod written as an RPCS3 patch is code the game runs from its first frame. This is the road the savefile helper takes in section 5.12.1, and it is the same here.

**Where the words come from.** MOD_PATCH, request `u8 index, u8 pad, u16 page` with the `index` of the mod's MOD_LIST row and the page wanted (revision 1.19; before it the request was `u8 index, u8 pad[3]`, whose zeros are page 0). All big-endian, laid out as SAVEFILE_PATCH's reply with `pages` where its first pad was:

```
u16 n       number of word rows on this page
u16 pages   how many pages the mod takes, at least 1, the same on every page (revision 1.19; 0 before it)
u32 stamp   the same on every page: CRC-32 of one unbounded reply after this field, below
{ u32 addr, u32 value }[n]                 be32 words
u16 nb      number of single bytes on this page: all of them on the last page, 0 on the others
u16 pad     0
{ u32 addr, u8 value, u8 pad[3] }[nb]      single bytes
```

Every cave comes first, in `patch.txt` order, each as consecutive words of its `.bin` - the whole file, streamed exactly as MOD_LOAD streams it into a console's memory - and then the mod's own words, in `patch.txt` order. That is the order a console writes them in, caves before the branches into them, and the order a patch file should list them in. A row's `value` is the four bytes the game holds at `addr`, read as a big-endian word, so a patch entry of type `be32` writes it as it stands.

A cave whose length is not a multiple of four ends in one to three bytes that make no whole word. They are not padded into one, which would write bytes the mod never names: they are the byte section, one entry each, every cave's tail together and sorted by address. NPEA00386's barlow_joba_trainer is the shipped example: its 534-byte `rack.bin` is 133 rows and two bytes. A patch that writes the rows and then the bytes, in reply order, puts exactly what MOD_LOAD would into the game. Should two entries ever name the same byte, the later one wins; no shipped mod does that.

**Dependencies are not flattened.** MOD_PATCH describes one mod and nothing it depends on. A client walks `#- depends` itself - it names other mods by their `#- name` - writes each mod as its own patch entry, and decides for itself what to do when two mods write the same address. NPEA00423's il-ghost depends on dl-cs; its reply is its own 745 rows, and dl-cs's 253 words are dl-cs's own reply.

The **stamp** is the ordinary reflected CRC-32 SAVEFILE_PATCH, SAVEFILE_LIST and `qwark.sum` use, over what one unbounded reply would hold after it: every word row of the mod, then `u16 nb, u16 pad` with the whole mod's `nb`, then every byte row. It is the same on every page. For a mod that fits one page that is everything after the stamp exactly as it is on the wire, the definition before revision 1.19, so such a mod's stamp has not changed. A client keeps it beside the patch entry it wrote and knows from it alone whether that entry is still the mod's current contents.

MOD_PATCH reads the mod table and the mod's files and nothing of the game, writes nothing, and answers in every session state MOD_LIST answers in. Since revision 1.18 it exists only in the emulator builds, `qwark-rpcs3.exe` and the `qwark-host.exe` simulator: the PS3 module answers it `UNSUPPORTED` whatever the payload, where it used to answer it for information, and never looks for a mod in the game (section 3.2). In the emulator builds it answers:

- `NOT_FOUND` for an index that is not in the list, as MOD_LOAD does for a mod it does not have
- `IO_ERROR` for a mod that failed to parse (flags bit4), as MOD_LOAD does, and for one whose `.bin` is missing, unreadable or empty
- `UNSUPPORTED` for a mod with a Lua automation (flags bit2): part of it is a script, and a patch cannot carry that
- `BAD_ARG` for a payload shorter than its four bytes, and for a `page` at or past the mod's `pages` (revision 1.19)
- `FULL` only for a mod whose byte rows alone would not fit one page, below. A cave has at most three tail bytes and a title at most 32 caves, so no mod qwark can parse comes near it. Before revision 1.19 `FULL` was any mod whose reply would not fit one frame; the size of a frame is no longer a reason for it

**Paging** (revision 1.19). A reply is one frame, at most `QWARK_MAX_PAYLOAD` = 16384 bytes, so a page carries at most (16384 − 8 − 4) / 8 = **2046 rows**, word rows and byte rows together. Take the whole mod as W word rows and B byte rows, in the order above. Then `pages` = ⌈(W + B) / 2046⌉, and at least 1. Page k carries word rows 2046·k up to 2046·(k+1) or W, whichever comes first, so every page but the last is full unless the words run out. The last page carries every byte row after its words; since W + B ≤ 2046·`pages`, it has at most 2046 − B words, and it may have none. A mod of 2045 words and 2 bytes, for example, is page 0 with the 2045 words and page 1 with the 2 bytes; one of exactly 2046 rows is one full page. A `page` at or past `pages` is `BAD_ARG`. Counted in what a mod is made of, its words plus a quarter of its caves' whole-word bytes plus its caves' tail bytes may come to 2046 before it takes a second page: a mod with no words fits 8184 bytes of cave on one. The largest shipped mod, NPEA00423's il-ghost, is a 2968-byte cave and three words, 745 rows on one page, and the largest in the old racman library, NPEA00387's coolcam, a 6312-byte cave and seven words, is 1585 rows on one; a 9.3 KB cave and sixteen words, about 2344 rows, is two pages, and answered `FULL` before this revision.

**What a client does with the pages.** It asks for page 0, then pages 1 to `pages` − 1 of the same index, concatenates the word rows in page order and takes the byte rows from the last page; together they are exactly the one unbounded reply described above, rows in the same order. Should any page's `pages` or `stamp` differ from page 0's, the mod changed underneath it (an upload and a MOD_RESCAN in between): it throws the pages away and starts again. qwark keeps nothing between the requests: every page reads the mod's files again and lays the whole mod out, keeping only its own slice, and the layout depends on nothing but those files, so the pages agree for as long as they do not change. A client written before revision 1.19 sends page 0 and reads `pages` as a pad. For a mod that fits one page it gets exactly the reply it always did. For a bigger one, which used to be `FULL`, it now gets page 0 alone and has no way to know there is more, so a client must read `pages` before it writes a patch; `qwark-rpcs3.exe` ships with its client, which is what keeps an older client from meeting this build.

**The presence rule.** On a platform that refuses code patches a mod counts as in the game when every byte of every cave and every one of its words reads back from game memory exactly as MOD_PATCH hands them out. qwark starts looking on the first INGAME tick and looks a little each tick - at most four reads of at most 2 KB a tick, a run of consecutive words being one read - so that no tick waits on the emulator for long; every mod of the shipped library is looked at within a few dozen ticks. While it looks, MOD_LIST says:

- bit5 CHECKING and not bit0: not looked at yet this session. The row changes by itself once there is an answer, so a client polls MOD_LIST.
- bit0 LOADED: all of the mod is there.
- neither: not all of it is there - a cave byte or a word differs, which is also what a patch from an older copy of the mod looks like - or its cave file is missing.

A mod that is never looked for never shows CHECKING: one that failed to parse, one with a Lua automation, and one with no words and no caves, which MOD_LOAD still loads, as nothing, exactly as it always has.

The answer holds for the session, because a patch goes in at load or not at all. It is looked for again when a game comes back, the same title included, since a patch written meanwhile counts from the next restart and not before, and after every MOD_RESCAN, since the files it was compared against may have changed: a client that uploads a new copy of a mod sees it CHECKING and then not LOADED until it rewrites the patch and the game is restarted. A read that fails, because the emulator is not answering, leaves the mod CHECKING rather than calling it absent. Outside INGAME no mod is CHECKING or LOADED on this platform. qwark writes nothing into the game for any of this, and SessionInfo's `mod_loaded` stays qwark's own loads, so it never carries a patched-in mod.

**What the client does with the reply.** It uploads the mod exactly as for a console - the files, `qwark.sum`, MOD_RESCAN - asks for it with MOD_PATCH, every page of it (revision 1.19), writes it into RPCS3's per-title patch file `patches/<TITLE_ID>_patch.yml` as its own entry, `be32` rows and `byte` entries, enables that entry in `patch_config.yml`, and has the user restart the game. Taking a mod out is disabling the entry and restarting. That half is the client's; nothing in qwark touches RPCS3's files.

### 5.7.2 Mods on a USB drive (revision 1.24)

A module may leave this feature out (3.1.2): then `flags2` bit0 is clear and MOD_USB_LIST is UNSUPPORTED. Build 56's release module does.

A console with a USB drive in it may carry mods there, available to load but not installed. qwark lists them and copies one into the console's library, `/dev_hdd0/qwark/mods/<TITLEID>/<dir>/`, only when it is loaded or its auto-load is turned on; from then on it is an installed mod like any other, in MOD_LIST, auto-loading, reapplied and unloaded as before. Module build 56; SessionInfo `flags2` bit0 USB_MODS says the module has this. qwark never writes to a USB drive.

**Where.** For each USB mount point the console gives storage, `/dev_usb000`, `001`, `002`, `003`, `006` and `007`, two places, in this order:

```
/dev_usb00N/qwark/mods/<TITLEID>/<dir>/      the console library's own layout
/dev_usb00N/mods/<TITLEID>/<dir>/            the same without the qwark folder, for a drive laid out by hand
```

`<TITLEID>` is the running title's, as for the library. A folder is a mod when it holds a `patch.txt`; a folder name longer than 31 bytes is skipped, as MOD_LIST and MOD_LOAD cannot carry it.

**One mod, several copies.** A mod is its folder name. When more than one drive, or both places on one drive, hold the same `<TITLEID>/<dir>`, one copy is listed and the rest are left aside: a copy that can be taken before one that cannot (BROKEN, below), then the newer version by the rule below. Copies of equal version are the same mod, and so are two with no version to compare: the first found is kept, which is the lower port, and on one drive `qwark/mods` before `mods`. Copies on USB are never a CONFLICT with each other; only the one kept is compared with the console's.

**When the drives are read.** At each game boot, just before the auto-load; at MOD_RESCAN; at MOD_USB_LIST and before a MOD_LOAD or MOD_SET_AUTO, when the last read of this title is more than a second old. Never on a tick, and never in the background: qwark does not watch for a drive going in, and a list asked for after one did is what sees it. The reads run under the core lock, on the tick thread or the asking connection's, and read every listed mod's `patch.txt` and `.bin` files to hash them, so a slow drive (a hard disk spinning up) holds the tick that long.

**MOD_USB_LIST** (0x0067), no request, any session state. `UNSUPPORTED` where code cannot be patched (section 3.2). The reply is `u8 n, UsbMod[n]`, at most 32 rows:

```
UsbMod (120 bytes, laid out as Mod):
u8   port        N of /dev_usb00N
u8   state       0 AVAILABLE   only the USB drive has the mod
                 1 SAME        the console has this very copy
                 2 NEWER       an update: the next load of the mod takes it
                 3 OLDER       the console's copy is newer, and stays
                 4 CONFLICT    the same version, or no version to compare, and other contents:
                               the console's copy stays
                 5 BROKEN      this copy cannot be taken; `problem` says why
u8   flags       bit0 WAITING: NEWER while the console's copy is loaded in the game, so the
                      update waits for the mod's next load
                 bit1 ROOT_MODS: the copy is under /dev_usb00N/mods, not /dev_usb00N/qwark/mods
u8   problem     for BROKEN: 1 patch.txt unreadable or over 16 KB, 2 a .bin it names is missing,
                 3 a .bin name qwark will not copy (absolute, "..", '\' or ':', over 63 bytes),
                 4 a .bin over 256 KB or the mod over 1 MB, 5 more than 32 .bin files,
                 6 a read failed. 0 otherwise
u32  hash        the USB copy's content, by qwark.sum's rule (section 5.7)
char dirname[32]
char name[32]    #- name, or the folder name without one
char version[16] #- version
char author[32]  #- author
```

`state` is worked out against the console's library as the reply is built, so it follows an upload or an unload at once. The content compared for SAME and CONFLICT is the console copy's `qwark.sum`, or, for a copy with none (put there by hand), its files hashed by the same rule.

**The version rule.** A version is up to four numbers separated by dots, with an optional leading `v` and spaces around it, nothing else: `1.10` is newer than `1.9`, `1.0` and `1.00` and `1.0.0` are equal. Anything else - an empty version, `1.0-beta`, `final` - is no version to compare. A USB copy is NEWER only when both sides have a version and the USB one is greater, and OLDER only when both have one and it is smaller. Otherwise the contents decide between SAME and CONFLICT, and in both the console's copy stays: qwark never replaces a copy on the strength of a version it cannot read, and a missing version on either side is never an update. A client that wants a CONFLICT's USB copy deletes the console's copy (DIR_DELETE); the mod is then AVAILABLE, and a load takes it.

**MOD_LOAD** (as before, `char[32] dirname`) first does what the drives call for, then loads as it always has:

- a folder the console does not have and a USB drive does is copied in, and loaded. BROKEN answers `IO_ERROR`, as a parse error does, and a copy that fails answers its status (below) with nothing changed. An auto-load key config.txt still has for the folder name, from a copy deleted earlier, is cleared, so the mod does not come back auto-loading;
- for a mod the console has, and everything it depends on (`#- depends`, by `#- name`), a NEWER copy of each one that is not loaded is taken first. A copy that fails is logged and the console's copy loads; the load's status is the load's own;
- a dependency the console does not have, and a USB drive has under that `#- name`, is copied in and loaded first, its stale auto key cleared as above;
- **a mod that is loaded is never replaced.** Its patch words and the originals its unload puts back are in the running game, so its files stay as they are and a MOD_LOAD of it re-applies it as before.
