Its NEWER row says WAITING, the log says the update waits, and the next load after an unload, a reboot's auto-load or a PREVIOUS_REAPPLY takes it.

Outside a game nothing is copied: the load is refused `NOT_INGAME` as before. The auto-load at boot and PREVIOUS_REAPPLY load through the same path, so an auto-loaded mod picks up a NEWER copy at the next boot, before anything of it is in the game. A mod only on USB is never in the auto-load: auto-load acts on installed mods, so a mod deleted from the console and still on a drive is AVAILABLE again and loads only when somebody loads it.

**MOD_SET_AUTO** of 1 for a folder only a USB drive has copies it into the library first, without loading it, and sets the flag; a copy that fails answers its status, BROKEN `IO_ERROR`, and a folder neither place has `NOT_FOUND`. For an installed mod it is unchanged and takes nothing: a NEWER copy of an auto-load mod is taken by its next load.

**The copy**, all or nothing:

1. `patch.txt` and every `.bin` it names, once each, the names checked as above, are copied from the drive into `/dev_hdd0/qwark/usbtmp/new/`, keeping any subfolder a `.bin` name has; each file is read back and compared, length and CRC, and the content hash of what was read from the drive must still be the one the list showed. A failed open or read - a drive pulled out, which is how one shows - a file grown past the bounds, or a hash that moved stops the copy, removes the temporary folder and leaves the library as it was; the next list or load reads the drives again. A cave's `.bin` the copy needs is never left out, so a mod with one missing is BROKEN rather than copied half.
2. `qwark.sum` is written beside them with that hash, eight lowercase hex digits, exactly what a client's upload writes, so MOD_LIST's `hash` for a copy taken from USB is the hash a client computes over the same folder, and a client holding the same files sees nothing to upload.
3. The console's copy, if there is one, is renamed to `/dev_hdd0/qwark/usbtmp/old`, the new folder renamed into its place (the old one renamed back if that fails), and the old one deleted. A power cut between the two renames leaves the mod out of the library and its copy in `usbtmp`; the drive still has it, and it lists as AVAILABLE.

Only `patch.txt` and the `.bin` files are copied, as only they are uploaded by a client; other files in the folder stay on the drive. The library is rescanned after a copy, which keeps every mod at its index (section 5.7). Each copy, each update that waits, and each load that keeps the console's copy over an OLDER or CONFLICT one is a line in `qwark.log`.

**What a client does with it.** It reads MOD_USB_LIST beside MOD_LIST and DIR_LIST when `flags2` bit0 is set, shows an AVAILABLE mod as on the console's USB drive, and loads it with MOD_LOAD by its folder name, uploading nothing. A NEWER row is an update to show; WAITING, one that comes with the next load. A client whose own library holds the mod as well should not upload its copy over a newer one, or the console takes the USB copy back at the next load, or keeps a newer console copy it was about to overwrite: the client the user ships uploads its own copy only when its version is not older than the USB copy's and the console's, by this rule.

### 5.8 Files (0x007x)

Paths are absolute, at most 511 bytes, sent as the remainder of the payload with no terminator. Only paths under `/dev_hdd0/` and `/dev_usb` are accepted. These run on the network thread and never touch game memory.

These are the transport of both libraries: the client uploads a mod's files with them (section 5.7), and it uploads, mirrors, renames and deletes savefiles with them (section 5.13). What a savefile no longer travels this way for is a *load*: the console keeps the file and copies it into the aside buffer itself.

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0070 | FILE_OPEN | `u8 mode` (0 read, 1 write and truncate), `path` | `u32 handle` |
| 0x0071 | FILE_WRITE | `u32 handle, bytes` (at most 16380 since revision 1.11, 65536 before it) | none |
| 0x0072 | FILE_READ | `u32 handle, u32 len` (len is taken as 16384 when larger, since revision 1.11) | up to `len` bytes; fewer at end of file |
| 0x0073 | FILE_CLOSE | `u32 handle` | none |
| 0x0074 | FILE_DELETE | `path` | none |
| 0x0075 | DIR_LIST | `path` | `u16 n, { u8 type (0 file, 1 dir), u8 namelen, u32 size, char name[namelen] }[n]` |
| 0x0076 | DIR_CREATE | `path` | none. Creates missing parents |
| 0x0077 | DIR_DELETE | `path` | none. Recursive |
| 0x0078 | USER_ID | none | `u32 user_id` |
| 0x0079 | FILE_RENAME | `u16 from_len, char from[from_len], char to[rest]` | none (revision 1.10) |

**DIR_LIST** fills one reply frame and stops: it has always broken off at whatever the reply buffer held rather than paging, and since revision 1.11 that buffer is 16384 bytes, so a folder of about 1400 short names is where the listing now ends instead of about 5700. Every folder qwark's own libraries use — the mod folders under `/dev_hdd0/qwark/mods/<TITLEID>/` and the savefile categories under `/dev_hdd0/qwark/savefiles/<TITLEID>/` — is orders of magnitude smaller than that, and the two library listings a client actually reads, MOD_LIST and SAVEFILE_LIST, are bounded by their own tables and fit whole. A client browsing an arbitrary `/dev_hdd0` folder with thousands of files in it will see the listing cut short.

**FILE_RENAME** (revision 1.10) moves a file within `/dev_hdd0`. Both paths obey the rule above; the old path must exist (`NOT_FOUND` otherwise) and the new one must **not** (`BAD_ARG` if it does), because cellFs and Windows refuse to replace an existing destination while POSIX replaces it silently, and a rename that means one thing on a console and another in the simulator is worse than one that never overwrites anywhere. A client that means to replace a file deletes it first.

It sits at 0x0079 rather than at 0x0075, where a rename would naturally have gone: 0x0075 has been DIR_LIST since revision 1, and an opcode is never renumbered.

**ui.pak** (revision 1.23, build 55). The console menu's file, `/dev_hdd0/qwark/ui.pak` (section 9.10), is written with these ops like any other file. The module looks at it again at its next tick after one of them changes it: FILE_CLOSE of a handle FILE_OPEN opened for writing on that path, FILE_DELETE of it, FILE_RENAME to or from it, and DIR_DELETE of a folder above it, each once the op is done. SessionInfo `flags` bit7 then says what it found, in the next block a client reads (telemetry and GET_STATE carry the block the tick publishes four times in its 120 a second). The way to put it right without a moment of a half-written pak is the module update's: write `ui.pak.new`, read it back, FILE_DELETE `ui.pak` (`NOT_FOUND` when there was none), FILE_RENAME `ui.pak.new` to `ui.pak`.

### 5.9 Combos (0x008x)

A combo fires once when `pad_mask` equals its mask exactly, and re-arms when `pad_mask` returns to 0. Revision 1.31: that is the game's own pad, read out of its memory; the VSH's view that SessionInfo's `pad_mask` may carry (section 3) fires nothing. Mask 0 disables the combo. Persisted in config, one set of masks for the console rather than one per title; since revision 1.24 a settings profile may override one combo at a time for its user (COMBO_OVERRIDE, section 5.10.1).

Actions: 0 SAVE_POSITION, 1 LOAD_POSITION, 2 DIE, 3 LOAD_PLANET, 4 LOAD_SETASIDE_FILE, 5 OPEN_MENU (revision 1.22), 6 TOGGLE_FAST_LOADS (revision 1.23). LOAD_SETASIDE_FILE makes the game load whatever is in the savefile helper's aside buffer (section 5.12), and does nothing in a game that has no helper, or where code cannot be patched and no RPCS3 patch put the helper in (section 5.12.1). OPEN_MENU opens the console menu, or closes it when it is up (section 9).

**TOGGLE_FAST_LOADS** (revision 1.23, build 55) turns the running game's Fast loads toggle on when it is off and off when it is on: RaC1's 0, RaC2's 0, UYA's 38, Deadlocked's 2, through FEATURE_SET's own code, so it is exactly what a client's checkbox sends, toggle_state, auto flag and refusals included, and a client sees the change in its next packet. A combo has no reply, so the console shows a notification at every press: "Fast loads on", "Fast loads off", or why not. Where code cannot be patched it gets FEATURE_SET's answer, `UNSUPPORTED` until the code switches are in (section 5.12.2), and says "Fast loads: needs qwark's RPCS3 patches"; any other refusal says "Fast loads: refused" and goes to the log with its status. It has no mask out of the box (`combo.fastloads`): Fast loads is a setting a runner picks once, the obvious buttons are the other combos', and a press of the wrong ones mid-run would change load times. The COMBO_ENABLE switch holds it off like the first five.

**OPEN_MENU** (revision 1.22) differs from the first five in two ways:

- It has a mask before anybody sets one: **0x060F**, L1 + R1 + L2 + R2 + L3 + R3, which a hand cannot press by accident in play. COMBO_LIST reports it whether or not config.txt names it, and COMBO_SET of 0 turns it off for good, like any other combo. It is a shortcut: with it at 0 the menu still opens from the Help row of the game's pause menu (section 9.2).
- Where the session has no menu - a game with no renderer, RPCS3, `menu.enabled = 0`, or another mod on the renderer's hooks - it shows a console notification saying why, once a session per reason, and does nothing else.

Revision 1.22 gave it a third difference, which revision 1.23 (build 55) takes away: COMBO_ENABLE did not hold it off, so a user who switched the combos off for a run kept the quick way into the menu. The pause menu's Trainer row is there to open the menu from (section 9.2), so the switch holds OPEN_MENU off like every other combo, and so does COMBO_SUSPEND, so a client captures it like any other. It keeps its default mask and COMBO_SET still rebinds it. With the switch off, and with `menu.help` at 0 as well so that the pause menu's Help row is the game's own, the menu has no way in until one of the two is turned back on.

COMBO_LIST therefore has **six** entries since revision 1.22, where it had five, **seven** since revision 1.23 and **fourteen** since revision 1.34. A client reads `n` and takes what it knows: the client shipped before a revision draws its own rows and looks each one up, so an entry it does not know is simply not drawn. COMBO_SET of an action past the module's last is `BAD_ARG`, which is why a client offers a row only against a build that has it.

**The page combos** (revision 1.34, build 59): actions 7 to 13, `COMBO_PAGE_*` in proto.h, one per page of the Trainer's root and the same list in every game, never renumbered (a new page goes on the end): 7 Game, 8 Cheats, 9 Planets, 10 Manips, 11 Unlocks, 12 Mods, 13 Save files. Each names its page by the label of the root's row that opens it, a section of the game's menu table (Game, Cheats, and Tools of Destruction's Manips) or a page of qwark's (Planets, Manips, Unlocks, Mods, Save files), so one list serves the four renderers and Tools of Destruction's pause menu alike (`menu.c`, `menu_page_combo`). COMBO_LIST lists them after the first seven, and after its entries names them: `u8 m, { u8 action, u8 len, char name[len] }[m]`, in the Trainer's order, a name at most 15 characters with no NUL; a client takes the page list and its names from there and from nowhere else. No mask out of the box, and an unbound one is no line in config.txt (`combo.page.game` .. `combo.page.saves`; COMBO_SET of 0 takes the line out), so a console whose user binds none has a config.txt no longer than before. A profile's override works as for any combo, an override of 0 included. A press:

- has OPEN_MENU's rules: the arming rule, the COMBO_ENABLE switch and the COMBO_SUSPEND hold, and OPEN_MENU's refusals said on the console once a session per reason (no menu in this game or under RPCS3 for the four games, `menu.enabled = 0`, another mod on the hooks, no ui.pak, Tools of Destruction's helper missing). It does nothing while a load the menu closed for is waiting, while the game's own planet picker the menu opened is up, and in Tools of Destruction while the galactic map qwark opened is opening or up;
- with the menu down, posts the page (the root under it, the cursor on the page's row) and asks the renderer to open, the request OPEN_MENU makes; an open nobody takes is withdrawn half a second on as ever, and the root is posted again then, so the game's own Trainer row opens on the root. A request that was taken but did not bring the menu up within four seconds (Tools of Destruction's pause opening first) puts the root back the same way;
- with the menu up, goes to the page at once and asks for nothing. Back from the page is the root with the cursor on that row, and Back again or a close leaves as from any page;
- for a page the running game's root has not (Mods where no mod can go in, so under RPCS3; Save files without the library), opens nothing, says "Trainer: no <page> page in this game" on the console once a session, and logs every press.

Tools of Destruction (section 9.12): the open is the combo's own, a press of the pause the game's pause test takes, so the pause shows its list for the frames the glue needs, then the Trainer opens on the page: a MAIN page in the pause's list, a CONFIG page (Game, Cheats, Manips, Planets' list, a save category) on Game Options. Its Planets combo is the root Planets row's action: in play the galactic map opens straight away with no pause (the row's CLOSES action, run at once since the game already plays), and Back from the map is the Trainer's root with the cursor on Planets, as from the row. Under RPCS3 with the Lua helper's patch the page combos work as on a console, Mods excepted.

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0080 | COMBO_SET | `u8 action, u8 pad[3], u32 mask` | none |
| 0x0081 | COMBO_LIST | none | `u8 n, { u8 action, u8 flags, u8 pad[2], u32 mask }[n]`; `flags` (revision 1.24, a pad byte before it) bit0 OVERRIDDEN, section 5.10.1; since revision 1.34 then `u8 m, { u8 action, u8 len, char name[len] }[m]`, the page combos' names |
| 0x0082 | COMBO_SUSPEND | `u8 suspend` (1 hold every combo off, 0 resume) | none |
| 0x0083 | COMBO_ENABLE | `u8 on` (1 combos fire, 0 every combo is held off) | none |

COMBO_ENABLE (revision 1.12) is the user's switch, the one the old RaCMAN had. Send 0 and no combo fires again until a later 1, whatever the masks say; any value other than 0 or 1 is `BAD_ARG`. It is not a game write, so it answers in any session state, exactly as COMBO_SET does: both do the same thing, which is write a key into `/dev_hdd0/qwark/config.txt`. The key is `combo.enabled`, it is 1 for a config that has never mentioned it, and it has the same scope as the five mask keys — one switch for the console, not one per title — so it survives a client restart, a game reboot and a console power cycle without the client storing anything. A hand edit picked up by CONFIG_RELOAD counts as a change like any other.

There is no op to read it back, because there is no need for one: `flags` bit3 COMBOS_OFF in the session info block (section 3) is set while the switch is off, and HELLO, GET_STATE and every telemetry packet carry it. COMBO_LIST is unchanged and still reports every mask whether the switch is on or off. Revision 1.22 spared OPEN_MENU from it (above); since revision 1.23 the switch holds off every combo.

COMBO_SUSPEND (revision 1.8) exists for capture. A client that records a combo reads the buttons out of telemetry's `pad_mask`, which is the same pad the console is watching, so without the hold the press that records "load position" also loads a position. Send 1 when the capture starts and 0 when it commits, is cancelled or is dropped; it is not a game write, so it answers in any session state.

The hold expires by itself two minutes after the COMBO_SUSPEND that set it, and is dropped when the session leaves the game. A client that dies mid-capture therefore cannot leave the combos off for good, and a client that resumes at the two-minute mark just sends 1 again. Lifting a hold does not re-arm anything: a combo still held when the hold ends fires only after the pad has returned to 0, which is the ordinary arming rule.

The switch and the hold are independent, and either one on its own holds every combo off: a combo fires only while the switch is on and no hold is in force. Turning the switch back on under a live hold changes nothing until the hold ends, and a hold expiring while the switch is off changes nothing either. Re-enabling re-arms as little as lifting a hold does — a combo whose buttons are still held when the switch comes on fires only after the pad has returned to 0.

### 5.10 Config (0x009x)

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x0090 | CONFIG_RELOAD | none | none. Re-reads `/dev_hdd0/qwark/config.txt` after a user edited it |
| 0x0091 | CONFIG_SAVE | none | none. Config is also written on every change |
| 0x0092 | MENU_HELP | `u8 on` (1 the console menu's Trainer row takes the game's Help row, 0 the Help row is the game's own) | none (revision 1.23) |
| 0x0093 | PROFILE_INFO | none | 64 bytes, section 5.10.1 (revision 1.24) |
| 0x0094 | PROFILE_ENABLE | `u8 on, u8 setup` | none (revision 1.24) |
| 0x0095 | COMBO_OVERRIDE | `u8 action, u8 set, u8 pad[2], u32 mask` | none (revision 1.24) |
| 0x0096 | RUN_WARN | `u8 on` (1 the console warns at a run start, 0 it says nothing, since revision 1.33 2 it warns about the cheats alone) | none (revision 1.30, section 5.10.2) |
| 0x0097 | TELEMETRY_RATE | `u8 hz` (30, 60 or 120: how often the telemetry packet goes out) | none (revision 1.32, section 5.10.3) |
| 0x0098 | REBOOT_APPLY | `u8 mode` (0 Always, 1 Ask, 2 Never: what a same-game reboot does with the previous-session record), or nothing to read it | none, or `u8 mode` for a read (revision 1.33, section 5.10.4) |

Config keys the module reads for itself: `log` (1 by default) writes a line per event to `/dev_hdd0/qwark/qwark.log`; `log = 0` silences it, which costs a file open, write and close per line on the console. `menu.enabled` (revision 1.22, 1 by default) is the console menu's own switch: `menu.enabled = 0` writes no renderer into any game, even when the menu combo is pressed, and takes out one already in at the first safe moment, the game's Help row its own again (section 9.2). No op writes it; a user edits the file, and CONFIG_RELOAD or a reboot picks it up. Revision 1.26, in a module whose games are modules (a test build, and a release from build 57): `game.floor_kb` (128), `game.window_ms` (3000) and `game.keep` (0), the memory gate and what happens to a module at the XMB (section 9.11); no op writes them either.

**MENU_HELP** (revision 1.23, build 55) writes `menu.help`, the console menu's Help row: 1 when the file does not mention it, the Trainer row in the game's pause menu where Help was, as build 54 always had it; 0, the game's own Help row in every game, with the rest of the renderer in and the menu combo opening the menu as before, while the combos are on. It is a config write like COMBO_ENABLE, answered in any session state; any value other than 0 or 1 is `BAD_ARG`, and a platform that cannot patch code, which has no menu, answers `UNSUPPORTED` and writes nothing (revision 1.29: but for a build with Tools of Destruction, whose menu works under RPCS3). In a game whose renderer is in, the tick thread puts the Help row's words in or takes them out at the first moment it is safe, and only then (section 9.2), so the change shows the next time the player opens the pause menu, or the next time after this one if it is open. SessionInfo `flags` bit6 MENU_HELP_OFF is set while the key is 0; there is no op to read it back. A hand edit picked up by CONFIG_RELOAD counts as a change like any other. `menu.enabled = 0` takes every word out whatever `menu.help` says.

Keys a client's own ops write. Console-wide rather than per title: the seven combo masks `combo.save`, `combo.load`, `combo.die`, `combo.planet`, `combo.setaside`, since revision 1.22 `combo.menu` (0x060F when the file does not name it) and since revision 1.23 `combo.fastloads` (COMBO_SET), and since revision 1.34 the page combos' `combo.page.game` .. `combo.page.saves` (COMBO_SET; written only while bound), `combo.enabled` (COMBO_ENABLE, revision 1.12, 1 when the file does not mention it), `menu.help` (MENU_HELP, revision 1.23, 1 when the file does not mention it), `telemetry.hz` (TELEMETRY_RATE, revision 1.32, 60 when the file does not mention it since revision 1.33, 30 before), `run.warnings` (RUN_WARN, revision 1.30, 1 when the file does not mention it), `reboot.apply` (REBOOT_APPLY, revision 1.33, 1 when the file does not mention it) and `selected.slot` (POS_SELECT). Per game since build 53: `selected.<game>.planet` and `selected.<game>.planet_flags` (PLANET_SELECT), `<game>` being `rac1`, `rac2`, `rac3` or `rac4` as in the manips' keys (section 5.14), so the disc trilogy keeps three. Until build 53 the planet selection was one pair for the console, `selected.planet` and `selected.planet_flags`; qwark never writes those two again, and a game whose own key the file does not hold yet reads the old one in its place, so the first PLANET_SELECT in each game is where its own pair begins (section 5.5). Each key is written to the file the moment the op lands, so none of them needs a CONFIG_SAVE to survive a power cycle, and CONFIG_RELOAD picks up a hand edit to any of them without a game restart. A write the console has no room for answers `FULL` and changes nothing (5.10.1, Room); since build 56 FEATURE_SET_AUTO says so too, where it used to answer OK and keep nothing. With settings profiles on (revision 1.24, section 5.10.1) the per-user ones among them, the selections, the manip options, `menu.help` and the auto flags, are the active profile's and live in its own config.txt; the combo masks and `combo.enabled` stay the console's.

### 5.10.1 Settings profiles (revision 1.24, build 56)

A module may leave this feature out (3.1.2): then `flags2` bits 1 to 3 and byte 30 are 0, and PROFILE_INFO, PROFILE_ENABLE and COMBO_OVERRIDE are UNSUPPORTED. Build 56's release module does.

A console can have several users, and each can keep settings of their own: a **profile**. Today a profile is a console user's; a later revision adds named profiles, picked from a client, beside them, which is why the layout and the ops talk about profiles rather than users.

**Off out of the box.** config.txt's `profiles.enabled`, console-wide, is the switch: 0 when the file has never named it, and then nothing is read from a profile and everything below is as before revision 1.24. PROFILE_ENABLE writes it; a hand edit picked up by CONFIG_RELOAD takes effect at the next second the console spends at the XMB.

**The layout.** `/dev_hdd0/qwark/profiles/<folder>/config.txt`, the same `key = value` text as the console's, and `/dev_hdd0/qwark/profiles/<folder>/positions/<game>.txt`, laid out like `/dev_hdd0/qwark/positions/`. A console user's folder is `user-` and the XMB's eight-digit user number, `user-00000001` for the first user, as `/dev_hdd0/home/00000001` is theirs. Named profiles will be `name-<slug>`. A profile's file holds only per-user keys and combo overrides.

**Which side every key is on.** With a profile active:

| Side | Keys and files |
|---|---|
| Per user: read from and written to the active profile only | `selected.slot`, `selected.<game>.planet`, `selected.<game>.planet_flags`, the old `selected.planet` and `selected.planet_flags`, `manip.<game>.auto_reset`, `manip.<game>.remember`, `manip.<game>.category`, `menu.help` (In-game menus), `run.warnings` (the run-start warnings, revision 1.30), `reboot.apply` (apply toggles on game reboot, revision 1.33), `<TITLEID>.auto.<slug>` (a toggle's auto-apply, and since revision 1.24 a COLOR kept for the boot, section 5.3.5), `<TITLEID>.mod.<dirname>.auto` (which mods load by themselves), `profile.*` (the profile's own), and the position slots |
| Combos, one at a time | the seven masks `combo.save`, `combo.load`, `combo.die`, `combo.planet`, `combo.setaside`, `combo.menu`, `combo.fastloads`, and since revision 1.34 the seven page combos' `combo.page.game`, `combo.page.cheats`, `combo.page.planets`, `combo.page.manips`, `combo.page.unlocks`, `combo.page.mods`, `combo.page.saves`, are the console's, in config.txt, for everyone. The active profile may hold a key of the same name, that combo's **override**, which wins for that combo alone |
| The console's, always config.txt | `profiles.enabled`, `combo.enabled` (the combo switch), `menu.enabled`, `log`, `savefile_helper`, `telemetry.hz` (the telemetry rate, revision 1.32), and any key not named above |
| The console's files, shared by everyone | the mod library (`mods/`), the savefile library (`savefiles/`), `ui.pak` and `qwark.log` |

A per-user key the active profile does not hold is unset, so it reads its default: it does not fall back to the console's value. While no profile is active (the switch off, or nobody signed in yet), the console's config.txt holds and answers every key, the per-user ones included, as before.

**Which profile is active.** The module asks the XMB for the signed-in user once a second while it is at the XMB, and only while the switch is on; never in BOOTING, INGAME or QUITTING, so a profile never changes under a game. When the user differs from the active profile's, the slots go back to the file they came from, the new profile's config.txt and slots are read, the toggles' and mods' auto flags are read again, and `profile_seq` moves. While nobody is signed in (the user-select screen), whatever is active stays. PROFILE_ENABLE acts at once, in a game too, since it is the user's own request.

**A profile that has never been made** has no folder. It reads every per-user key as its default, and nothing is made for it until something is written into it: its first per-user write, slot store or combo override makes the folder and an empty config.txt, and then the write. Nothing is ever copied into a profile but by PROFILE_ENABLE's setup, so a user who signs in later with no profile starts from the defaults. A folder that cannot be made answers that write `IO_ERROR` and leaves the profile unmade.

**PROFILE_INFO** (0x0093), no request, 64 bytes, answered in XMB and INGAME:

```
u8   flags      bits 1-3 as SessionInfo flags2 (bit1 PROFILES, bit2 PROFILES_ON, bit3 PROFILE_ACTIVE),
                plus bit5 HAS_PROFILE: the signed-in user's profile has been made, whether or
                not the switch is on. Bits 0, 4, 6 and 7 are 0
u8   kind       0 the console's settings are in use, 1 a console user's profile, 2 reserved for
                named profiles
u8   seq        profile_seq
u8   pad
u32  user_id    the signed-in XMB user number, 1 for 00000001; 0 when nobody is signed in
char folder[24] the signed-in user's profile folder, "user-00000001", whether or not it is in use;
                "" when nobody is signed in. NUL-padded
char name[32]   reserved for named profiles: all 0 for a console user's
```

On a platform without users (qwark-rpcs3) every byte is 0.

**PROFILE_ENABLE** (0x0094), `u8 on, u8 setup`, no reply. `on` writes `profiles.enabled`. `setup`, which acts only with `on` 1, and only on a profile that has not been made (HAS_PROFILE clear): 0 nothing; 1 **COPY**, make the signed-in user's profile with every per-user key of the console's config.txt and the four position files (`rac1` .. `rac4`); 2 **FRESH**, make it empty. A profile that exists is never overwritten: the setup is ignored. `on` > 1 or `setup` > 2 is `BAD_ARG`, and `UNSUPPORTED` where flags2 bit1 is clear. Turning the switch off leaves every profile folder where it is, so turning it on again finds them. A client asks the question once, as the user turns the switch on: PROFILE_INFO first, and when HAS_PROFILE is clear, "copy the current settings, or start from the defaults?", then PROFILE_ENABLE(1, 1 or 2); otherwise PROFILE_ENABLE(1, 0).

Since build 56's fix PROFILE_ENABLE is all or nothing. The switch is written first, so a config.txt with no room for it answers `FULL` and nothing changes. A setup that cannot finish (a folder or file that cannot be written, `IO_ERROR`; a copy the module cannot hold, `FULL`) takes away every file it wrote, puts the switch back as it was and answers that status, with no profile active, so the next PROFILE_INFO still says HAS_PROFILE clear and the question is asked again. `profile_seq` moves either way.

**Room.** The module holds config.txt's keys and the active profile's in one pool of 12 KB, a key costing its own length rather than the widest one's: the largest config.txt qwark itself writes (every fixed key, the auto flag of every toggle and colour under every title id the four games answer to, and 32 mods with 31-character names auto-flagged in each game) is 231 keys and about 10.4 KB of it (revision 1.34: 238 keys and about 10.6 KB with all seven page combos bound, about 31 bytes each; none of them costs anything unbound). While a profile is active the console's own per-user keys are not held: they stay in config.txt, whose every write carries them over from the file it replaces, so a COPY does not need the room twice. A write the pool has no room for answers `FULL` with nothing changed. A file the module cannot hold all of, larger than the pool or than 16 KB of text (a hand-edited one), loads what fits, CONFIG_RELOAD answers `FULL`, and it is never written back: every write to it is `FULL` until it is trimmed and reloaded, rather than the file being cut down to what was read. Until this fix the store was a table of 64 keys: a console with more loaded the first 64, the next write dropped the rest from the file, one over 6.6 KB of text loaded nothing and the next write replaced it with the one key it set, and a new key, `profiles.enabled` among them, was `FULL`.

**COMBO_OVERRIDE** (0x0095), `u8 action, u8 set, u8 pad[2], u32 mask`, no reply. `set` 1 stores `mask` as the active profile's override of that combo (0 too: the combo is off for this user); `set` 0 removes it, so the console's mask is in force again, and removing one that is not there is `OK`. An action past the last combo, or `set` > 1, is `BAD_ARG`; `UNSUPPORTED` where flags2 bit1 is clear; `NOT_FOUND` when no profile is active. **COMBO_SET** writes the console's mask whatever is active, so under an override its change shows to this user once the override goes. **COMBO_LIST** answers the mask in force, the override where there is one, and sets that row's `flags` bit0 OVERRIDDEN. Overrides stay in the profile's file while the switch is off and are back when it goes on. COMBO_ENABLE's switch is the console's.

**profile_seq** (SessionInfo byte 30, and PROFILE_INFO's `seq`) moves by one whenever the active profile changes (a new user at the XMB, PROFILE_ENABLE turning the switch on or off, a setup) and at a CONFIG_RELOAD while a profile is active. A client that sees it move reads PROFILE_INFO, COMBO_LIST, MOD_LIST, POS_LIST and MANIP_LIST again. A single key write does not move it: the client that made it knows.

**Files.** Since build 56 config.txt, a profile's config.txt and every position file are written to `<file>.new` and then put in the old file's place (cellFs will not rename onto a file, so the old one is deleted first). A read that finds no file but a `.new`, which is what a write cut off between the two leaves, reads the `.new`, and the next write puts a whole file back.

Since build 57 a write of either config.txt reads the file it replaces first and writes its lines back in their order: a key the module holds with the module's value, the first time it comes (a second line for the same key goes), and every other line as it was, whether a comment, a blank, a key the module does not know, one longer than 63 characters or with a value longer than 31 (which the module does not hold, rather than hold cut short, so it reads as unset), or, while a profile is active, config.txt's per-user keys; the module's keys the file did not have follow. CR LF and a last line with no newline read like any other line, and the file goes back with LF. Only COMBO_OVERRIDE's Clear takes a line out, the override's. A file that is there but will not open (a console's cellFsOpen fails for a busy drive, too many open files or an FTP server holding it) is tried once more and is then `IO_ERROR`, not "no file": CONFIG_RELOAD answers it, the file is not written until a reload reads it, and the positions file the same, so that a read that failed never becomes a file with only the next write in it. A write is refused, the old file in place and no `.new` left, when the old file does not read, a write to the `.new` does not take all its bytes, or its close fails. Build 56's release module, and every build before this one, wrote the module's keys alone: a key it had not loaded, for whatever reason, was gone from the file at its next write.

### 5.10.2 Run-start warnings (revision 1.30)

As a run starts the console shows one notification (on a console the VSH's, which is all a player looking at the game sees) with a line for each thing on that the run should not have, and none at all when there is nothing:

```
Warning: N cheats enabled                    (1 cheat)
Warning: N mods enabled                      (1 mod)     mods marked #- legal: false
Warning: N unknown mods enabled              (1 unknown mod) mods with no #- legal: line, said once
```

in that order, each line left out when its count is 0. qwark-rpcs3 has no VSH: its line goes to its log (`notify:`), as every notification there does.

**The run start** is the autosplitter's START (section 8), the one moment all five games share: the core warns from the function every game's watcher emits its events through, never from a game. RaC1: game state 6 to 0 on Veldin. RaC2: the start state on Aranos. UYA: Veldin out of a load. Deadlocked: a load into the DreadZone station with the tutorial flag clear. Each of those emits RESET and START on the same tick, and the warning goes once, at the START. Tools of Destruction: the RESET goes out as a fresh run's load into Metropolis begins (`tod_fresh_run_load`, a file that has not beaten Metropolis), the START at the run's fade in, and the warning with the START, once the player can see the game. A death, a checkpoint, a reload of another planet and a quit emit no START, so they say nothing; a save loaded where the watcher sees a new run, which a runner does to restart, is a new run start and warns again.

**Cheats.** The running game's TOGGLEs that its own table counts (`struct game_describe`'s `cheats`, one bit per feature id; nothing is guessed from a group or a label) and that are on at that tick: toggle_state as qwark has it, a LIVE toggle read back from game memory on that tick, and under RPCS3 a WRITES_CODE toggle still waiting for the code switches. The counted toggles:

| Game | Counted (feature id) | On the Cheats page or beside it, not counted |
|---|---|---|
| RaC1 | Fast loads (0), Ghost Ratchet (3), Infinite ammo (1), Freeze health (2), Goodies menu (6) | Debug's Update Ratchet, Update mobys, Update particles (on is the game as it always is), Camera mode |
| RaC2 | Fast loads (0), Ghost Ratchet (3), Infinite ammo (1), Freeze health (2), Enable debug mode (5), Reset bosses on death (13), Reset platinum bolts on death (14) | |
| UYA | Fast loads (38), Ghost Ratchet (3), Infinite ammo (0), Freeze health (1) | Quick-select pause (5), one of the game's own options; Reset trophies on Veldin load |
| Deadlocked | Fast loads (2), Ghost Ratchet (5), Refill ammo (3), Freeze health (4) | Crash patches, Fix reset softlocks, Correct HUD brightness, Correct gradient brightness |
| ToD | Fast loads (17), Infinite ammo (8), Infinite health (9), God Ratchet (16), Remove black screens (24) | Remove cutscenes (run-legal), Cache cutscenes on reset, Disable cutscene caching |

Since revision 1.34 a row the game declares run-legal (Feature flags2 bit0 RUN_LEGAL: Deadlocked's Fixes, UYA's trophy reset toggle, ToD's Remove cutscenes and cutscene cache toggles) is never counted, whatever `cheats` says, and the module's tests hold every table to having no row in both.

Fast loads counts in every game: each lists it under Cheats, and no category of the five is known to allow it.

**Mods.** The mods in the game (MOD_LIST's LOADED: qwark's own load, or under RPCS3 what the presence look found) with `#- legal: false`, and those of unknown legality (section 5.7). A mod with `#- legal: true` never counts.

**Once.** The unknown-legality line is said at the first run start a mod of unknown legality is in the game for, counting every such mod in the game then; after that it is said again only at a run start with one the module has not warned about yet (a mod added, or loaded for the first time), again counting them all. The module remembers each mod by its title and folder name until it stops, which on a console is the console's power-off: a game's reboot, a rescan, an unload and load again, or a switch to another game and back do not bring the line back. The cheats and the illegal mods are said at every run start they are there for.

**The switch.** RUN_WARN (0x0096), `u8 on`, writes `run.warnings`, no reply: 1 (the default) the console warns, 0 it says nothing, and since revision 1.33 2, cheats only: the cheats line and no mod line, the mods counted all the same (qwark's log has them). Anything else is `BAD_ARG`; a number other than 0, 1 or 2 put in the file by hand reads as 1. It answers in any session state and on every platform, and a run start reads the key afresh. While it is 0 or 2 no mod is remembered as warned about, so the mod lines, once back, warn about an unknown mod that came while they were away. SessionInfo `flags3` bit1 is set while it is 0, bit6 while it is 2, bit0 says the module has the warnings and bit5 that it takes 2; a client shows its switch only against bit0 and offers cheats only only against bit5. An older module refuses 2 with `BAD_ARG`; an older client reads a console set to 2 as on. `run.warnings` is a per-user key (section 5.10.1).

### 5.10.3 The telemetry rate (revision 1.32)

**TELEMETRY_RATE** (0x0097), `u8 hz`, no reply, writes `telemetry.hz`: how often the tick thread sends the telemetry packet (section 4) to every subscriber. 30 every fourth tick, 60 (the default since revision 1.33) every second tick, 120 every tick; anything else, a missing byte included, is `BAD_ARG` and writes nothing. A config.txt that does not name the key, or names another value by hand, sends at 60 Hz (at 30 Hz before revision 1.33, which a module before it still does). The tick reads the key again whenever the config has changed (this op, CONFIG_RELOAD, a profile switch), so the new rate starts on the next tick; it lasts across games, reboots of the game and the module's restart, since it is in the file. It answers in any session state (XMB and INGAME; BOOTING's BUSY as for every config op, section 1.1) and on every platform: qwark-rpcs3 sends to its subscribers the same way. SessionInfo `flags3` bit2 says the module has it, and a client shows its control only against that bit; bits 3-4 are the rate in force (0 30 Hz, 1 60 Hz, 2 120 Hz).

The key is the console's, not a user's (section 5.10.1): one rate for every client connected, so a client that sets it sets it for the others too, and an older client, which never sends the op, receives packets at whatever rate the console has. Nothing else follows the rate: the autosplit datagrams go out every tick whatever it is (section 8.4), the combos and the console menu read the game's pad every tick, the LIVE toggles are re-read at 10 Hz, and the VSH's GetInfo2 is asked every 33 ms.

**What it costs.** The tick thread, not a network thread, encodes the packet and sends it: one SessionInfo encode and the watch table under the core lock (since revision 1.32 HELLO's copy of SessionInfo is taken out of the packet rather than encoded a second time), then one `sendto` per subscriber under the network mutex: a client subscribes once per connection, so four at most in practice (the table has room for eight, for a connection that subscribes two ports). A packet is 169 bytes with no watch and at most 937 bytes with 64; at 120 Hz to four subscribers that is 81 KB/s, or 450 KB/s with every watch slot in use (900 KB/s for eight), which the console's network carries easily. The tick already sends the autosplit datagram every tick while an event is repeating, so a send per tick is a cost the module has carried since revision 1.4.

### 5.10.4 Apply toggles on game reboot (revision 1.33)

When a game reboots into the same title, what was on in the last session goes into the previous-session record (section 4.1): the toggles, mods, freezes and client patches that were on, less the toggles and mods flagged on boot (a toggle's `<TITLE>.auto.<slug>`, FEATURE_SET_AUTO; a mod's `<TITLE>.mod.<dirname>.auto`, MOD_SET_AUTO; the console menu's on-boot rows). qwark applies those as the game's code arrives at every boot, a reboot or not, silently, whatever this setting says. config.txt's per-user key **`reboot.apply`** decides what becomes of the record as the game arrives:

| Value | Mode | At a same-game reboot |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | Always | qwark re-applies the record itself, as a PREVIOUS_REAPPLY of every category this platform can do (toggles and freezes everywhere, mods and client patches where code can be patched), with or without a client connected. Nothing is left pending |
| 1 | Ask (the default, and what any other number in the file reads as) | with a client connected (a TCP connection open to the module), the record waits behind `flags` bit0 PREVIOUS_PENDING for the client to ask the user and answer with PREVIOUS_REAPPLY or PREVIOUS_DISMISS, as every build before revision 1.33 did. With no client connected Ask is Never: the record is dropped, so a client that connects later is never asked about a reboot it did not see, and the console itself asks nothing |
| 2 | Never | the record is dropped; nothing is re-applied or offered |

A boot that is not a same-game reboot (the first boot of a title, or another title in between) has no record, so it offers and re-applies nothing in any mode. The qwark log says which way each reboot went.

**REBOOT_APPLY** (0x0098), `u8 mode`, writes `reboot.apply`, no reply: 0, 1 or 2; anything else is `BAD_ARG` and writes nothing. With no byte it is a read and the reply is `u8 mode`, the key as the module reads it, so a client can show the setting (it is in no SessionInfo bit). A config write like RUN_WARN's: it answers in any session state and on every platform, and the next reboot reads it. Per user (section 5.10.1): a client reads it again when `profile_seq` moves.

SessionInfo `flags3` bit5 SETTINGS_133 says the module has the op; a client shows the setting only against it. A module before revision 1.33 sends 0 there, answers the op `UNKNOWN_OP` and keeps every record for whichever client connects. A test build of the revision had `boot.apply` instead, which held back the on-boot toggles and mods, with BOOT_APPLY at 0x0098 meaning that, BOOT_ASK at 0x0099 and `flags3` bit7 BOOT_ASK. `boot.apply` is not read now (a line of it stays in the file, as any unknown key does), 0x0099 is `UNKNOWN_OP` and bit7 is 0.

### 5.11 Autosplitting (0x00Ax)

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00A0 | AUTOSPLIT_EVENTS | `u32 since_seq` | `u32 latest_seq, u8 n, Event[n]`: every event with `seq > since_seq` that the 64-entry ring still holds, oldest first (`since_seq` 0 = all of it); `latest_seq` is 0 before the first event |
| 0x00A1 | AUTOSPLIT_DESCRIBE | none | `u8 n, EventDesc[n]` (32 bytes a row since 1.5) for the running game. UNSUPPORTED when no game is running or the game has no watcher |

AUTOSPLIT_EVENTS answers whatever the session state is: a client that reconnects after a crash still wants the splits that happened while it was away. AUTOSPLIT_DESCRIBE is gated on INGAME the same way DESCRIBE is, because under BCES01503 the running game is not known until the fingerprint answers.

### 5.12 Save files (0x00Bx), revision 1.9

| Op | Name | Request | Reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00B0 | SAVEFILE_INFO | none | `u8 supported, u8 installed, u8 running, u8 pending, u32 size, u32 done, u32 total, u8 error, u8 why, u8 pad[2]` (20 bytes since revision 1.10; `why` since revision 1.29, pad before it) |
| 0x00B1 | SAVEFILE_READ | `u32 offset, u32 len` (len at most 16384 since revision 1.11, 65536 before it) | the bytes of the aside buffer at that offset; short at the end |
| 0x00B2 | SAVEFILE_WRITE | `u32 offset, bytes` (at most 16380 since revision 1.11, 65536 before it) | none |
| 0x00B8 | SAVEFILE_PATCH | none | `u16 n, u16 pad, u32 stamp, { u32 addr, u32 value }[n], u16 nb, u16 pad, { u32 addr, u8 value, u8 pad[3] }[nb]`: the running game's helper as the words and bytes a patch writes (revision 1.15, section 5.12.1). `UNSUPPORTED` from the PS3 module (revision 1.18) |

INFO, READ and WRITE answer **NOT_INGAME** outside INGAME. READ and WRITE answer **UNSUPPORTED** where the platform cannot patch code (RPCS3, `flags` bit2) unless an RPCS3 patch put the helper in the game when it loaded; INFO answers there either way (revision 1.15, section 5.12.1). Before revision 1.15 the three answered UNSUPPORTED on that platform ahead of the INGAME test, so it said UNSUPPORTED at the XMB too; it says NOT_INGAME there now, as a console does. None of the three installs the helper: that is done by the set-aside and load actions, by RaC1's Force autosave, and by a library STORE or RESTORE with a valid file, and a client never asks for it or sees it happen. INFO, the library's metadata ops and raw READ and WRITE of the aside buffer only ever look.

Since revision 1.10 READ and WRITE are the **debug and test path**, not what a save or a load does: the library ops of section 5.13 move a whole file between the console's own filesystem and this buffer without any of it crossing the wire. They still work exactly as they did, and a client that wants the bytes on the PC still reads them here.

- **supported** — 1 when qwark has a helper for the running game. 0 is an OK answer, not an error: it is how a client knows to hide its save-file panel. All four games are 1 today.
- **installed** — 1 when qwark has written the helper into this process. A supported game reports 0 until an action installs it, and while it is 0 so are `running` and `pending`, since the helper's request bytes are not read from a process that has no helper in it. Where code cannot be patched (revision 1.15) it is 1 when an RPCS3 patch put the helper in the game at load, and 0 when nothing did or what is there does not match; qwark writes nothing there, so it is the only way it is ever 1 on that platform (section 5.12.1).
- **running** — 1 when the helper's own byte reads 1. The helper writes it on every call, so this says the code is installed *and* that the game is reaching the hook. It is 0 for the first frame or two after an install, and it stays 0 for as long as the game is on a screen that does not run the hooked routine.
- **pending** — bit0: a set-aside request is still outstanding. bit1: a load request is still outstanding. bit2 (revision 1.10): qwark is copying between a file and the aside buffer, section 5.13. The helper clears its own request byte when it has done the work, so a client polls this rather than guessing at a delay.

  **A clear bit means the work is over, not that the byte happens to read 0.** Build 11 made that true at both ends. In the game, the helper clears the set-aside byte *after* its copy loop rather than before it, so the byte reading 0 is the copy having finished; it copies a megabyte or two in 32 KB steps, and clearing first meant a client could read the aside buffer while the copy was still walking it and save a file with a torn tail. In qwark, a bit goes up the moment the request byte is written and stays up until the byte has read 0 on **every tick of a settle window**, 30 ticks of the 120 Hz loop, a quarter of a second (`SAVEFILE_SETTLE_TICKS` in `src/core/savefile.h`). The window is there because one read of one byte of a running game is thin evidence: the helper puts no barrier between the copy and the store that clears the byte, and a memory read that fails answers with a zero of its own. A byte that goes back to non-zero inside the window starts it again.

  A client should therefore poll INFO until the bit clears and only then read (or, for a load, only then consider the load done). The quarter of a second is the price of the guarantee, on an operation a user waits seconds for.
- **size** — how many bytes the aside buffer holds, which is the size of a save file for that game. It is fixed per game and does not depend on the save.
- **done**, **total**, **error** (revision 1.10) — the transfer of section 5.13. `done` and `total` are its bytes, and `error` is why the last one stopped: 0 none, 1 file missing, 2 io error, 3 the file is not exactly `total` bytes, 4 the game went away underneath the copy, 5 the aside buffer was already spoken for, 6 (build 43) the game was not somewhere a save could be taken or put back, 7 (revision 1.20, build 53) there was no save to act on, 8 (build 56) the helper could not be installed because `/dev_hdd0/qwark/ui.pak`, which carries it, is missing, damaged or another build's (below). All three describe the **last** transfer until the next STORE or RESTORE starts, so a client that polls once more after `pending` bit2 clears reads how it ended rather than zeroes. Since build 43 `error` is also why a set-aside or a load was refused, and since build 53 a set-aside or a load clears it as it starts, as a STORE or a RESTORE always has: it is the reason for the last of the four, and a request that goes out, or is refused for a reason with no code here (the `BUSY` of an emulator qwark cannot read yet, say), leaves 0 rather than an earlier refusal's reason. A set-aside or a load asked for while a STORE or a RESTORE runs is `BUSY` with error 5 (build 53): it would write over the save being stored, or hand the game a buffer still being filled.
- **why** (revision 1.29) — which of the game's checks `error` came from, set and cleared with it: 0 nothing more to say; with error 6, 1 not in gameplay (the game state, or no level up), 2 a planet load queued or under way, 3 the title screen, 4 the game's save system saving, loading or deleting (or an autosave about to start), 5 the game's save system not up, 6 the game did not take the load within its wait, 9 a save or load of the game's own started under the copy; with error 7, 7 the file (or the buffer) is not a save of this game, 8 the game has not saved into a slot or loaded one since it started. Only a game whose save system qwark drives itself sets it, Tools of Destruction today (section 5.12.4); the others answer 0.

**Error 6, the game was not idle (build 43).** A set-aside or a load only means anything while the game is sitting still. Ratchet: Deadlocked keeps working on its own save data from inside the game — the load is a menu, the autosave writes the same buffer, and every planet load, lobby screen and cutscene transition moves the game between modes that read or rewrite it — so a request raised across one of those either copies a save the game is still writing or hands the loader a buffer something else is about to walk over, which is how a stored file came back with the game's fingerprints in it. qwark now asks the running game before it raises either request, and answers `BUSY` with error 6 without writing anything into the game, so a client shows a reason at the moment the user pressed the button rather than a request that quietly comes to nothing. Since build 53 RaC1, RaC2 and RaC3 are asked too, with tests of their own (below). For Deadlocked the test is the game's own: `gameMode` must be 0 (ordinary play) or 3 (the pause the savefile helper parks the game in itself), and `pendingGameMode` must be -2, meaning no mode change is queued. The embedded helper makes the same test on the frame it would act, because qwark's tick and the game's frame are different clocks; a request it finds busy there is **dropped, not deferred** — a load that fired at some later moment the user did not ask for would be worse than one that plainly did not happen — and it leaves a code in the request byte that qwark clears and reports as error 6 in turn.

**Deadlocked's load waits for its own pause (builds 44 and 45).** The helper puts the game in its pause to run the load menu, and it runs part way through a frame, so the frame it pauses in still finishes and anything that frame queued - an autosave above all - takes the game out of the pause for a while and back. Build 43 handed the save over four frames after pausing regardless, and what the game did next went on writing into the save it had just been given. The helper now hands it over only once the game is in its pause with no mode change queued, on a later frame than the one it paused in (it keeps the game's own frame counter for the mode, gameModeTimer, from the pausing call), waiting up to ten seconds for that. Build 44 also made the game sit in the pause for fifteen frames in a row first; build 45 drops that quarter of a second. The game back in ordinary play before then means the pause is gone, and the load is dropped rather than raised later; running out of time drops it too and puts back the mode the pause replaced. Either way qwark reports error 6, as for a load the helper refused outright. While the load waits, `pending` bit1 stays up. A cancel in the menu waits the same way (build 45): the helper used to put the old mode back on the frame it saw the cancel, and the game's own close of the menu can queue a pause after that, which left the game paused with no menu. It now moves the load byte from 2 to 3 and puts the mode back on a later frame, once the game is in play or in its pause with nothing queued, and only if it is still paused; the byte goes to 0 then, so `pending` bit1 stays up through the cancel too.

**RaC1's, RaC2's and RaC3's loads go from the game's pause (build 53).** The three games' loaders write every field of the save over the live game the moment they run, the current planet first, while the planet's own data stays the old planet's until the planet load that follows. Up to build 52 the helper loaded wherever the request found the game, and RaC1 then updated and drew the rest of that frame with the new planet's number over the old planet's world: the world render picks a planet's sky routine by that number, and a Veldin save loaded on some planets drew sky layers the loaded sky did not have, through a null pointer. RaC3 had not been seen to crash and goes the same way as a precaution.

None of the three has a mode that runs nothing. Each has a freeze of sorts - RaC1's prompt, and in RaC2 and RaC3 the mode the game's mode enum calls freeze (4), a message over a stopped game - but it draws the world under the message, which is the render that crashed. The pause alone neither updates nor draws the world, so the helper loads from the pause, and it takes the game there itself, from whatever the game is doing and with none of the Start button's own tests:

- **RaC1** changes state by writing the state word, from dozens of places, and the helper does the same: it enters the start menu (state 3) through the game's own leaf routine for that (0x13D3E0, which the title uses), which also puts back the start menu's own words - the screen, the pending action and three more - so the menu does not repeat its last close; the screen is then set to the one the Start button opens.
- **RaC2** has its pause written the same way. Its helper runs last in the frame, after the frame's update and render, and everything the game runs after it in that frame reads the mode word afresh, so the helper writes the pause into it with no mode change queued behind it and loads in the same call: what is left of the frame is the pause's, the loader sets the load request, and the planet load follows at the end of that frame. No frame of the pause runs, so the pause menu is never entered. Before it writes the mode it puts away what a cutscene leaves behind that the planet load does not, the way the Start button's skip does: the movie player's Bink file is closed, the cutscene's queue of movies and scenes is emptied, the callback the cutscene's end would run is dropped without running, and a movie held under the console's freeze is let go; leaving the vendor, it also gives the renderer back the setting the vendor's entry zeroed, which nothing but the vendor's exit restores. Up to this change the helper asked mode_requestChange for the pause as RaC3's still does, and a load during a cutscene still failed on hardware: the request is refused while the cutscene has its next movie, scene or end queued, and once the change is made it runs the movie's or the scene's exit, which runs the cutscene's callback - a moby's continuation, which can itself start the next planet's load, after which the load was refused.
- **RaC3** changes mode through the game's mode change, which at the end of the frame runs the leaving mode's exit (a movie stopped, a scene ended, the vendor closed) and the pause menu's entry. Its helper runs first in the frame, and the frame then runs the update and render of the mode it began in whatever the helper writes, so writing the mode word is not enough there: the helper asks mode_requestChange for the pause as the Start button does (mode 3, pushed, screen 1, or screen 5 on the vid comics), and asks again on its next call while the game refuses for now - a mode change already queued, the player with no health, the game blocking its pause for the moment.

RaC1 and RaC3 load on the first call from a later frame that runs in the pause: the state or mode reads the pause (with nothing queued) and the game's own frame counter for it has moved since the call that entered it. A load asked for while the player is already in the pause goes at once. The planet load that follows starts the new planet in its ordinary opening mode - RaC1's main loop starts every planet in its arrival state, RaC2's and RaC3's mode stacks are reset and the new planet starts in ordinary play - so the pause ends with the load. `pending` bit1 stays up while the load waits, up to ten seconds by the console's clock (the timebase; until this change 600 calls of the helper, which came to five seconds or less with two controllers in); a load still waiting then, or one that finds the game somewhere it may not be paused while it waits, is dropped and reported as error 6, and leaves the player in the game's own pause. A RaC2 load never waits: the helper either refuses it at once or loads it.

What is left for **error 6** is a load the pause cannot be entered for at all, refused before anything is raised: a planet load already on its way (all three), and in RaC2 a trip already decided, the ship's mode queued or under way; RaC1 playing a movie, which runs in a frame loop of its own until it ends, or in a state its main loop runs nothing in; RaC2 on the title screen, or while the console holds the game in its own freeze (the PS button's menu, a lost controller), whose latch only the game's own release clears; RaC2 and RaC3 in space, the trip between planets, or in a mode past 9 (RaC3's lobby and multiplayer modes); RaC3 in debug mode, which its mode change traps on leaving. RaC2's debug mode, refused until this change for the same trap, loads now: its helper never leaves a mode through the mode change. The helper makes the same tests on its own frame.
qwark makes the same tests before it raises either request, so most refusals are a `BUSY` at once. A load also needs either a save in the aside buffer or, with the aside buffer empty, a save of the game's own to fill it from; a set-aside needs only that save, wherever the game is, since it copies the game's last save and not its live state. Without them the answer is error 7 (revision 1.20). The aside buffer is empty when its first word is 0, which is what every game's helper takes for nothing set aside, filling the buffer from the game's save instead - but only until qwark itself has written into it in the process, by a RESTORE's copy or SAVEFILE_WRITE (build 53). After that a first word of 0 is what the user put there and no save, so a load of it - the LOAD_ASIDE action, the combo, or the load a RESTORE raises after its copy - is refused with error 7 in all four games, Deadlocked included, before anything is raised; until build 53 such a RESTORE loaded the game's own last save and reported success. A save is recognised as the games' own restores recognise one, by the sizes of its blocks at its head (RaC3's are 0x37B0 and 0x84C0, or RaC2's older pair). RaC1's, RaC2's and RaC3's LOAD_ASIDE actions force fast loads on, and are refused before they do; a request that fails as it goes out puts back its own force, a load the helper drops on the game's frame puts them back on the next tick, and the end of the load puts back the checkbox's setting as a planet load's end does (build 53 for RaC1 and RaC3). In RaC1 and RaC2 a drop puts back only the dropped load's own force (build 53): once a PLANET_LOAD has gone out after it the force is that load's, and a RESTORE's load, which forces nothing, puts back nothing when it is dropped, whatever a planet load under way forced. RaC1's load is over once its request word is back at 0 and its game state has left 6, the planet transition's and the new level's own, because one of the fast-load sites runs in that state after the request word is cleared - and only once the request word has been seen up since that load went out (build 53): a set-aside load asked for in a planet's fly-in, the state still 6 from the planet load, is taken by the helper into the start menu, state 3, which until then read as the planet load's end and put the fast loads back before the set-aside load was raised.

**RaC1's request bytes and aside buffer moved, and RaC2's helper (build 53).** RaC1's request bytes were the mod's 0xB00070..0xB00073, inside the game's bump arena, and its aside buffer 0x1000000..0x10B0000 ended over game variables from 0x10A0230 on. Both live in a static buffer of the game's, 0xFB0230..0x10A0230: the aside buffer at 0xFC0000, still 0xB0000 bytes so every save file keeps its size, and the bytes at 0x1080000 (`api_mod`, `api_load`, `api_setaside`, Force autosave's byte at 0x1080003). One routine writes that buffer: the update function of moby class 1750 (0x2B1D98, registered for class 0x6D6 by the class map at 0x2A3B8), which serialises a save into it, 0xA4A14 bytes and never past 0x1054C44, on every frame such a moby updates; this paragraph called it a debug menu's command until the review that followed. The request bytes are clear of it. The aside buffer is not: in a level with that moby its first 0x94C44 bytes become the level's own save from 0xFDD0 on. The mod's buffer had the same exposure over its first 0x54C44 bytes, so it is not new in build 53. What class 1750 is, and which levels place one, is level data the executable does not hold. RaC2's helper was at 0x22A368, the head of a routine planet Joba runs every frame, so it ran in Joba's place there; it is in dead library code at 0x113EC64 now, still reached by a `bla` from the same hook site, and its request bytes and aside buffer did not move. RaC3's helper stays at 0x980AC0 with the same hook word, request bytes and aside buffer, but it grew with the pause, and it shares its dead function with the Infinite ammo (until build 53 Freeze ammo) code switch at 0x980F00 (section 5.12.2): the build now fails a RaC3 helper that would run into the switch, as it already did for RaC1's and RaC2's caves. Its two words for the wait are at 0xD9FF14 and 0xD9FF18, zero-filled data a patch need not write. It also carries UYA's trophy reset (section 5.14): a second cave at 0x9808C0..0x980AC0, the top of the strictly dead code below its own, and a third at 0x980308..0x9807F0 for the thread the reset runs on, which the build fails either of if it outgrows it (only 0x980304..0x980308 and 0x9807F0..0x9808C0 stay free of the helper; the console's fast-load cave takes 0x9807F0..0x9808B0 of the second, section 5.12.3), and a fourth request byte, `api_trophy` at 0xD9FF1C, which a console install zeroes with the other three, with the helper's busy byte for the reset beside it at 0xD9FF1D. SAVEFILE_PATCH's byte entries stay the three request bytes: the fourth is zero-filled data too, and nothing writes it where code cannot be patched. RaC1's, RaC2's and RaC3's SAVEFILE_PATCH words are all new, so a patch written by an earlier build for any of the three reads as one from another build (section 5.12.1) and the client writes it again; Deadlocked's did not change by a byte, and its patches still count.

A client built against revision 1.9 reads the first eight bytes and is right about every one of them; the three new fields are appended, and nothing before them moved.

**A save, end to end.** FEATURE_TRIGGER the game's SAVE_ASIDE action; poll SAVEFILE_INFO until `pending` bit0 clears; SAVEFILE_READ the whole buffer in chunks of at most 16384 bytes (16000 is the size a client that also talks to older builds should use). **A load** is the reverse: SAVEFILE_WRITE the file into the buffer in chunks from offset 0, in order, each write answered before the next goes out, then FEATURE_TRIGGER the LOAD_ASIDE action and poll until `pending` bit1 clears. The bytes are opaque; nothing on the PC knows the save format.

A client is expected to hold the whole save until it has every chunk before it writes a file, and to send a file only when its length is exactly `size`: the console cannot tell a short file from a whole one, and a save that is neither is what the game crashes on later.

Bounds: a READ whose `offset` is past the end is BAD_ARG and one that runs off the end comes back short, so a client can ask for a round chunk at every offset. A WRITE that would run off the end is BAD_ARG rather than trimmed, because a client sending more than the buffer holds has the wrong file.

**Where the buffer comes from.** qwark has a small piece of PowerPC code per game, compiled from one source in `src/games/sfhelper/` into the committed `src/games/sfhelper_bins.c`. Installing it writes one to three code caves and a branch word into the running game; from then on the game calls it once a frame, and it does nothing until a request byte changes. It is never reverted: taking a branch back out from under code that may be executing it is a crash, and with no request outstanding it costs a byte write and three comparisons a frame. Nothing is written to the console's filesystem at any point.

**The helper's bytes are in ui.pak (build 56).** Up to build 55 the module carried all four helpers, 6 KB that only ever go into the running game. The PS3 module now keeps each helper's numbers (its request bytes, its aside buffer, how many caves and hook words it has) and reads the caves and the hook word out of `/dev_hdd0/qwark/ui.pak`, the console menu's file (section 9.10), when it installs the helper: the game's entry whole, its CRC checked and its numbers held to the module's own, before a byte goes into the game, so the bytes that land are exactly the ones the module carried before. A pak that is missing, damaged or another build's installs nothing: the action that wanted the helper (a set-aside, a load, STORE, RESTORE, UYA's trophy reset) answers `BUSY` with `error` 8, the request byte is not raised, and SessionInfo `flags` bit7 says the pak is bad, so a client can send the right one and the user try again, in the same session. INFO still answers `supported` 1 and `installed` 0; the library's listing and file ops never needed the pak; a helper already installed in the process is not affected by the pak going away. RPCS3 never reads the pak: its helper comes from the RPCS3 patch (section 5.12.1), which the emulator builds still make from the bytes they link.

Before this revision the same two ACTIONs moved a `tempsave` file under `/dev_hdd0/game/<TITLEID>/USRDIR`, which the client then pulled over the FILE ops, and the helper was a mod the user had to load first. Both are gone. The FILE ops stay, for the mod library.

### 5.12.1 The helper under RPCS3 (revision 1.15)

qwark cannot install the helper under RPCS3: the emulator recompiles PPU code before it runs it, so a cave and a branch written into a running game are memory and nothing more (section 3.2). RPCS3 does apply its own patch files, though, and it applies them to the executable as it loads it, before it compiles anything. A patch that writes the helper's caves and hook words at load, and zeroes its three request bytes, is therefore code the game runs from its first frame, and the savefile block works on top of it exactly as it does on a console.

**Where the words come from.** SAVEFILE_PATCH, request none, describes the helper for the running game. All big-endian:

```
u16 n       number of patch words
u16 pad     0
u32 stamp   CRC-32 over every byte of the reply after this field
{ u32 addr, u32 value }[n]                 be32 words
u16 nb      number of single bytes, 3 today
u16 pad     0
{ u32 addr, u8 value, u8 pad[3] }[nb]      single bytes the patch sets at load
```

Every cave comes first, as consecutive words in address order and the caves in the order qwark installs them, then the hook words, which is also the order a patch file should list them in: a reader applying them one at a time never has a branch in place before what it branches to. A word's `value` is the four bytes the game holds at `addr`, read as a big-endian word, so a patch entry of type `be32` writes it as it stands. A cave whose length is not a multiple of four would have its last word padded with zero bytes; every cave today is whole instructions, so none is.

The single bytes are the helper's three request bytes, `api_mod`, `api_load` and `api_setaside` in that order, each with `value` 0: the three a console install zeroes before it hooks anything, because whatever the executable holds there would fire the moment the hook goes in. Under RPCS3 there is no install and the hook is live from the first frame, so the patch zeroes them at load instead. It mattered: until build 50 RaC2's three sat in its executable's code segment and loaded as 0x80 0xFE 0xA4, which without the zeroing read from boot as a set-aside and a load outstanding that never cleared, with the helper's refusal code in the load byte. Since build 50 every game's request bytes load as zero - RaC1's, RaC3's and Deadlocked's are in zero-filled BSS, and RaC2's are in a gap of the read-write segment that is zero in the file - and the byte entries stay, for the next executable that does not.

**RaC2's request bytes moved in build 50.** rc2-save, which the RaC2 helper comes from, kept `api_mod`, `api_load`, `api_setaside` and its copy counter at 0x10CD71D..0x10CD723, inside the executable's code segment, and the helper stores to `api_mod` on every frame. A console does not mind. RPCS3 does: it makes that segment read-only once it has applied its patches (section 3.2), so the first frame through the patched-in hook would have faulted the game. They are at 0x1317728, 0x1317729 and 0x131772A now, with the counter at 0x131772C, in the gap between the end of the TLS template and the next data at the top of the read-write segment (0x1317714..0x131773F), which is part of the segment's file data, reads zero and is referred to by nothing in the executable; the code switches' two RaC2 flag bytes take the start of the same gap. SAVEFILE_PATCH names the new addresses, and a patch written by an earlier build differs in the helper's own code as well as in its bytes, so it reads as not present and the client writes the new one.

The stamp is the ordinary reflected CRC-32 that SAVEFILE_LIST and `qwark.sum` use, taken over everything after it exactly as it is on the wire - the word pairs, the `nb` head and the byte entries - so it changes exactly when anything the patch writes does: a client keeps it beside the patch it wrote and knows from it alone whether that patch is still the current build's.

It reads nothing out of the game and writes nothing into it. Since revision 1.18 it exists only in the emulator builds, `qwark-rpcs3.exe` and the `qwark-host.exe` simulator: the PS3 module answers it **UNSUPPORTED** in every session state, where it used to answer it for information - on a console qwark writes those very words itself, on the first action that needs them - and never looks for a patched-in helper (section 3.2). In the emulator builds it answers **NOT_INGAME** outside a game, **UNSUPPORTED** for a game with no helper (a title qwark does not know included) and with the helper switched off by `savefile_helper = 0` in `config.txt`. Since build 53 RaC3's is the largest reply, 658 words and three bytes in 5300 bytes with its trophy reset and the reset's thread; Deadlocked's is 2132, RaC1's 2116 and RaC2's 1972, all well inside a frame.

**The presence rule.** On a platform that refuses code patches, the running game's helper counts as present when every byte of every cave and every hook word SAVEFILE_PATCH describes reads back from the game exactly as qwark would have written it. The single bytes are no part of it: the helper and qwark change them from the first frame on, so what they read later says nothing about whether the patch went in. qwark looks once a session, on the first INGAME tick, and keeps the answer until the session ends; it never looks outside INGAME, and it looks again when a game comes back, since a patch goes in at load or not at all. So:

- A patch written while the game is running counts from the next restart, and not before.
- A patch from an older qwark build differs somewhere - a cave byte, or a hook word that branches to where the old helper began - and reads as **not present**. The client writes the current words over it and the game is restarted again.
- A read that fails, because the emulator is not answering, leaves the answer unknown rather than "not present": INFO and every op that needs the helper answer **BUSY** until a look succeeds. The three library ops that answer on the network thread rather than the tick (CATEGORIES, LIST, CATEGORY) never read the game themselves: they take the answer the tick found, and are BUSY for the tick or so before the first look as well.

**When it is present** everything behaves as on a console after an install. INFO reports `installed` 1, `running` is the helper's own `api_mod` byte reading 1, and `pending` and the transfer fields are what they always are; READ, WRITE, the two actions, RaC1's Force autosave, the combo and the whole library of section 5.13 work. The install step writes nothing at all - not the caves and the hook, which are there already, and not the three request bytes a console install zeroes first either, because here the patch zeroed them at load, the hook has been live since the game booted, and those bytes are the helper's own. RaC1's, RaC2's and RaC3's set-aside loads force fast loads as they do on a console when the code switches' patch is in as well (section 5.12.2), through the fast-load flag, and go at the game's own pace without it; before revision 1.17 RaC2's always went at the game's own pace here.

**When it is not**, INFO answers with `supported` as on a console and `installed` 0, and everything that needs the helper answers UNSUPPORTED as it did before this revision. A client tells the platform apart by SessionInfo `flags` bit2, which it already reads: with bit2 set and `installed` 0, it offers to write the patch.

**What the client does with the reply.** It writes the words and the bytes into RPCS3's per-title patch file, `patches/<TITLE_ID>_patch.yml` under RPCS3's folder, enables that patch for the game in `patch_config.yml`, and has the user restart the game. That half is the client's; nothing in qwark touches RPCS3's files.

### 5.12.2 Code switches under RPCS3 (revision 1.17)

Nine toggles are instruction patches on a console, flagged `WRITES_CODE`: RaC1's Fast loads and Infinite ammo; RaC2's Fast loads and Infinite ammo; RaC3's Infinite ammo (Freeze ammo until build 53) and Fast loads; Deadlocked's Crash patches, Fast loads and Refill ammo; and since revision 1.29 Tools of Destruction's Fast loads, in the builds that have it. (RaC3's Fast loads were two data words, and needed no switch, until they became a code patch; see 5.12.3. RaC1's Infinite health was the tenth until build 53, when it became Freeze health, a freeze that writes no code; its switch is still in RaC1's patch, below, and nothing writes its flag.) qwark cannot patch code under RPCS3 (section 3.2), so on that platform the game is given both forms of every instruction those toggles change, at load, by an RPCS3 patch - the road the savefile helper takes in section 5.12.1. Each patched instruction, a *site*, becomes a branch to a *trampoline* in a code cave; the trampoline reads a *flag byte*, runs the patched form of the instruction when it is 1 and the game's own when it is 0, and branches back to the instruction after the site. The flag bytes are in data memory, which RPCS3 leaves writable, so turning a toggle on or off there is a byte write.

Deadlocked's crash patches include one word of data, the last word of a vperm control table at 0x808830, which RPCS3 makes read-only with the code around it. Its switch hooks the one instruction that loads the table instead, and with the flag set loads a copy of the patched table out of the cave.

On a console none of this happens: the toggles patch code as they always have, qwark never looks for the switches, and bit4 is never set. Since revision 1.18 none of it is in the PS3 module at all, and SWITCH_PATCH, which used to answer there for information, answers `UNSUPPORTED`; it exists only in the emulator builds, `qwark-rpcs3.exe` and the `qwark-host.exe` simulator (section 3.2).

**Where the words come from.** SWITCH_PATCH, request none, describes the switches of the running game. All big-endian, laid out exactly as SAVEFILE_PATCH's reply:

```
u16 n       number of rows
u16 pad     0
u32 stamp   CRC-32 over every byte of the reply after this field
{ u32 addr, u32 value }[n]                 be32 words
u16 nb      number of flag bytes
u16 pad     0
{ u32 addr, u8 value, u8 pad[3] }[nb]      the flag bytes, each with value 0
```

Every cave comes first, as consecutive words, caves in address order - Deadlocked's last is the sixteen-byte table its bot-crash trampoline loads - and then every site's branch word, in address order: a reader applying the rows one at a time never has a branch in place before the trampoline it branches to. A row's `value` is the four bytes the game holds at `addr`, read as a big-endian word, so a `be32` patch entry writes it as it stands. The byte entries set every flag byte of the game to 0, so every switch is off at every boot whatever the executable holds there; every flag is zero in the file today, and the entries are the belt to that. The stamp is the reflected CRC-32 of section 5.12.1, over everything after it as it is on the wire.

| Game | Rows | Flag bytes |
|---|---|---|
| RaC1 | 44: five trampolines (39 words) and five sites | 0x9058B0 fast loads, 0x9058B1 infinite ammo, 0x9058B2 the old infinite health, which nothing writes since build 53 |
| RaC2 | 33: four trampolines (29 words) and four sites, the crash guards' two included | 0x1317720 fast loads, 0x1317721 infinite ammo |
